PLEASANT PARAGRAPHS.

A COOKING SCHOOL.—Matrimony. "Taken from the French "—Alsace and Lor- tsine. The things that go without saying must have escaped feminine attention. THE charity which begins at home is often so discouraged that it never goes farther. Waring: "Does your wife tell you everything?" Barfoot : " Well—er—she tries to." WHEN silence is broken, "the least said, the soonest mended." THE party who shakes the tree doesn't always get the most apples. A WORD to the wise is generally sufficient to get the adviser into trouble. THE man who won't see his own errors is playing with himself with loaded dice. A THING of beauty is a joy, while it remains in fashion. .

"THAT air is very familiar," said the musician as a gust of wind took his hat away. TIME has more lives than a cat. It can stand an indefinite amount of killing. CONSIDERING how little the bell knows, it is wonderful how much it has tolled.

STILL TIMID.—" I've never had the courage to get married." "Haven't, eh? What's your busi- ness?" "Oh, I'm only a lion tamer." THE man who is willing to wait for something to turn up is usually too blind to see it when it comes along. REMARKED by the hands of the chronometer: "We haven't any eyes, but i've're always on the watch, just the same." "AND where is your husband working now, Mrs. Smith?" "He ain't working—he's got a post in a Government Office." JOHNNY: "Pa, can you tell me what fame is ! " Pa: "Frame, Johnny, is what a man gets for being civil to newspaper men." THE- worst cases of incompatibility of temper occur when both parties have the same kind of temper, and plenty of it. "Do you admire Professor Svengali's playing ?" "Oh, immensely! He plays such good accompani- ments for conversation, you know.' • SOME men give one the impression that if they were pricked with a pin there would be nothing left of them. HicEs : "I am never able to find anything in this house." Mrs. Hicks: "Oh, yes, you are. You generally manage to find fault." ACTRESS: " Don't'you think woman can do a deal toward elevating the stage?" Johnnie: "No! but—yes, she can lower her hat." How SHE KNEW.—Bridegroom "When did my darling first discover that she loved me ?" Bride (sweetly) : "When I found myself getting mad every time any one called you a fool." A HAMPSTEAD poet writes: "Why do I sit here in the gloaming ? ' We give it up. Did you step on an orange peel! JUDGE (to prisoner) : "Have you anything to add to your defence?" "No, my dear judge. Dispose of me as if it were your own case. • SHE THOUGHT IT SUPERFLUOUS.—An Irishwoman from an agricultural district seeing fish being washed for the first time in her life, very naïvely remarked that she thought it a useless operation to wash fish in water, seeing that they had lived in the water all their life! PUTTING IT NICELY.—Mr. Steckstein (presenting bill) : " Goot morning, Mr. Paynon. 1 came to tell you there's lots of people talking about you. They say you're a cheat, a liar, und t ief, und you don'd pay your bills. But I said, 'No, Mr. Paynon is an honourable gentlemans, und pays his bills.' Now, vich is it ?' SWEET WOMAN's LOGlC.—Husband: "Don't you think that you are rather unreasonable to expect me to take you to a ball, stay awake until four o'clock, and then get up at eight to go to my work ?" Wife: "I may be a little unreasonable, but it's perfectly brutal of you to mention it." "HERE is the lovliest house coat that I bought for Tom, and he doesn't seem to care for it the least bit." "I can tell you how to make him value it above everything." "Oh, how ?" "Tell him that you've given it away to some poor man at the door. 'POMPEY'S Pll.LAR.—Planter : "That's a vicious looking mule you!ve got there, Pompey." Pompey: "Dat mewel, sah ? Dat mewel am one ob de wondahs ob the world." Planter: "One of the wonders of de world ?" Pompey: "Yes, sah. Dat mewel spilled me oaten de waggon yessirday." Planter: "Is that 'is,hy you call him one of the wonders of the world ?" Pompey: "Yes, sah ; Pompey's spiller, you know. Hah ! hah ! Gid 'long dar !" So SUDDEN !—They were climbing up the moun- tain-side, and coming to a steep place he deemed it proper to assist her, and, turning, said, "Please give me your hand." "Oh," she replied, with a blush, "this is so sudden! You must ask papa." AN hotel proprietor in a French town had a notice put up, "English, Spanish, German, Italian Spoken Here." An American came to the hotel and asked in very good French for the inter- preter. "There is not one," replied the host. "But," said the. American, pointing to the board, "you say all those languages are spoken." The -answer was unique. "Yes, sir, by the visitors." MRS. NEWRICH : "I want to look at some -statuary for my library." Dealer: "How would you like 'one of these marble busts of Shakespeare ?" Mrs. Newrich : "Send my a dozen of him, and show me what else you have." GREAT INDUCEMENTS.—" I am deeply gratified at your determination to join the church," said the Rev Dr. Thirdly to young Mr. Trivvet. "Might I ask what decided you to take that step ?" " Cer- tainly you may,' replied Trivvet. Dr. Talmage says there will be three women to one man there." BEST UPPER LEATHER.—The following sound advice occurs in an almanack : "If you wish to make a shoe of durable materials, you should make the upper leather of the mouth of a hard drinker; for that never lets in water." FADDISH.—McCorkIe : "Isn't Briggs naturally a lazy man?" McCrackle : "Not exactly lazy; but he seems to think it is very unhealthy to work between meals." RATHER ANClENT.—Habitual Drunkard: "I trust your worship will excuse me this time. It is my misfortune. I am a child of genius." Magistrate: "And what is your age ? " H. D. : "Forty-two years." Magistrate : "Then it is time you were weaned. You'll have to do fourteen days away from the bottle." GETTING IT OFF HIS MIND.—"Now, John," she said, persuasively, "you will try to do those few errands for me when you get down town." "Yes, indeed," he replied. "Don't put them off." " No. I make it a rule now, when I promise to do anything for you, to get it off my mind as quickly as possible ' "Yes," she answered, with a gentleness that relieved the sarcasm, "you do— by forgetting it." HAD HIM THERE.—Joker (to friend) : "As to that, my boy, one year I drank nothing but milk." Friend: "indeed ! I have known you a long time, but I don't remember that. What year was ib ? " Joker: "My first ! " IT is only guileless boyhood that vows he "will never do it again." Even when caught in the act, the full-grown man ot sound mind tries to prove that he didn't do it at all. THE DIGNITY OF ART. —John Crome, the landscape painter "Old Crome," as he is affectionately called—died in 1821. On the day of his death he charged his eldest son, who was sitting by his bed, never to forget the dignity of art. " Johnny, my boy," said be, '' paint, but paint for 'fame ; and if your subject is only a pig- stye, dignify, it, Johnny, dignify it. A SMALL boy was detected by a farmer up his cherry tree. When he had made the boy come down he began to lecture him on the sinfulness of his action. The boy answered indignantly, "how, you just count thm cherries over again before you insinuate that I stole any of them." PUTTING IN A GOOD WoRD.—A Devonian, lec- turing on temperance, ended his discourse thus: "And, finally, my friends, why should any of you drink ardent spirits? My son Toin has got as good cider as any in the country, at sixpence a quart." As ASPIRATED PUZZLE.—Traveller (relating ad- venture) : "And the boat's screw being disabled, we were compelled to lay to." Listener: "Par- don me, did I understand you to say the boat's screw, or the boat'ri crew ?" Traveller (indig- nantly) : "I said the boat-screw. Is that plain enoufih ?" Listener: "Oh. yes ; thanks."

CAPITAL" CHAT.

It seems just a little maladroit to empliaise too strongly the fact that the genial "Autocrat of the Breakfast Table," who has just died in the fulness of days at Boston, was born in the same year as Mr. Gladstone, but by a fortunate coincidence, any ill-omened suggestiveness attributable to this re- minder is blunted by simultaneous news from Australia that the " Grand Old Man " of Melbourne has lived ten years beyond the age reached by Mr. Gladstone and Dr. Wendell Holmes. The birth of Dr. Macartney, Dean of Melbourne, takes us back to the last century. In six years more he would have seen the outgoing of the -old century, whose incoming he also witnessed. Although Dr. Macartney could not exactly look upon Mr. Glad- stone and Dr. Wendell Holmes as mere boys, ten years to an octogenarian makes a large difference, and we may well hope that Mr. Gladstone may fol- low his example of longevity rather than that of Dr. Holmes. The "Autocrat " is understood to have, spent his declining years in preparing his reminis- cences, and as his fame and loveableness attracted to him every notable person who at any time got within reach of Boston there should be a very entertaining bock in store.

A daring scheme is being matured for giving a new basis to the Londen coal trade, ostensibly as much in the interests of consumers as in those of coal owners. Instead of many firms competing against each other, it is proposed to unite them all in one trust under the title of the London Coal Commissioners, who would be a semi-Government body, arranging the whole trade of London and dealing directly with coal owners and consumers. It is calculated that a capital of a million would be required to purchase existing interests.but the pro- moters maintain that the citizens of London would be able to buy their coal in future at rates nearer in accord with those of more favoured localities— namely, at about thirty per cent. less than present prices. The large coal owners in the Midlands and the North are understood to be enthusiastic over it. Of course an Act of Parliament would be required, and every point in the scheme would be subject to very free criticism. The project is expected to see the light in detailed form in a very few days.

I had a curious experience in the Strand on Sun- day night. While walking eastwards, I noticed three gentlemen coming towards me. One, a tall man, dropped his umbrella, and another of the three picked it up with the exclamation, " Ah, monseigneur !" The speakers were the Due d'Orleans and M. Dupuy. The third man I did not recognise. To this state has the French Monarchy come—that its representatives may walk unheeded and hardly recognised on a Sunday evening in one of the busi- est streets of London, and this, too, at the moment when those amusing masqueraders, the English Jacobites,are boasting of swelling adhesions to their cause, and are fancying themselves fine fellows in toasting "The Queen over the Water." The fallen umbrella in Fleet-street reduces to very matter of tact dimensions the flamboyant talk of Jacobite clubs as to the prospects of the glorious cause of tegitimacy, whether in England, France, or Spain.

The Rev. Hugh Price Hughes will not return to town for some little time, but his friends here do not seem to attach much importance to the re- port that he contemplates abandoning his well- known work in West Central London. and under- taking a position in connection with the Wesleyan Missionary Society. Mr. Price Hughes is so ardently devoted to the mission of which—in con- junction with Mr. Mark Guy Pearse—he has had charge for several years that it is considered un- likely he will allow any but the most pressing con- siderations to induce him to withdraw from it.

The objections raised by certain London justices of the peace to the payment of five guineas as an "installation" fee have induced Mr. Asquith to fix the charge for the future at two guineas, and to cause the balance of three guineas to be refunded to those magistrates who have already paid the fee. This, however, is not considered satisfactor3, and on Tuesday the London County Council passed a resolution urging the abolition of the charge, on the ground that it puts a serious obstacle in the way of poor men accepting nomination to the magisterial bench.

Mr. Windsor, who is possibly one of the best known of Chinese merchants, has been in this country for a holiday, and sailed on Wednesday on his return. He has had twenty-six years experience of the Celestial Empire, and speaks the language. It is his steamers which carry on the coasting trade of Dhina, and he is a persona grata to the Viceroy, Li Hung Chang. Speaking of the war, he tells me that he has every confidelice in Chinese resources to recover the disasters of the present conflict, and that in the end not China, but Japan, will suffer. The Chinese he regards as a much misunderstood race, and confirms the impression that the war will begin a new epoch in the history of China. Defeat will have an effect which victory would not have had, and he believes we shall see the Chinese advancing towards Western civilisation as rapidly as the Japanese have done. The end, he is certain is not yet. Unless Japan strikes at once, she will be too late. In a fortnight the winter will have set in, and ice will close the ports of the Gulf of Pechili. This will give the Chinese time to refit their fleet, and meet the Japanese at sea on equal tern- s once more with much experience gained.

For the first time in history the population of the United Kingdom is greater than that of France. It is computed that at the present moment there are in the British Isles one hundred thousand more people than there are in France. During the cen- tury the population of France has increased by ten millions; that of the United Kingdom by twenty millions. In addition to that we have in those ninety years colonised Australia, New Zealand, South Africa, and other spots which in another ninety years will become mighty nations. The now steady decrease in the population of France is not accompanied by any colonisation whatever.

The Rev. John McNeil,whose services in the Dutch Reformed Church a few months ago attracted large congregations appears to have created something of a sensation in Melhourne. The Argus of that city, referring to Mr. McNeil's services says :—" Mel- bourne is witnessing just now a curious social phenomenon. A preacher who, though well-known is scarcely to be described as famous, and who has no pretensions to shining scholarship or startling novelty of views, is conducting a series of meetings and the largest buildings in the city are too small for the crowds which are attracted. The church were Mr. lcN all is about to conduct a service, even at the busiest hour of a week-day, resembles noth- ing so much as a besieged city.

The heir to the earldom, Mr. Albert Grey, is now in his 43rd year, and is a nephew of the deceased earl. He has not had much experience of public life. He did not enter upon a political career until his 27th year, when he contested South Northum- berland in the Liberal interest at a by-election. He stood at the head of the poll ; but two of the votes given for him were rejected, and this led to the double return of himself and his opponent,in conse- quence of an equality of votes. Both caneidates took seats in the House, but Mr. Grey ultimately retired in order to try his luck at the impending general election. His good fortune gave him an overwhelmning majority, and the brief taste of ,! Parliamentary life which he then experienced con- stitutes the whole of his political record. Joining the Liberal Unionists at the next e!ection, he lost his seat, and has been living in political retirement ; since the great split of 1880 He has not, however, I quite withdrawn himself from public affairs, to some of which he has given much attention. His I Parliamentary career was too brief to permit him to make any mark in the 'House, but he can look back to a .satisfactory career at Cambridge, and the Uni- versity Extension question has enlisted his warmest 'interest since.

A man named Ravel!, his three sons, and a family of five persons living next door, have been killed at Iron- wood, Michigan, owing to the explosion of a piece of dynamite which Ravelt was attempting to thaw on a cooking stove. -

_ Mr. William Exley, a well-known Shin!ev gentleman. was on Tuesday morßing found dead on his bedroom floor under circumstances pointing to suicide with a gun. Deceased's brother was on the same day removed to a lunatic asylum.

LADIES' LETTER.

DEAREST NELLIE, There is quite a rage for white pique ornaments on the jaskets of cloth and serge dresses, but I "lust warn you against the mistake that is some- Imes made of imagining that a single set may be Worn with a variety of costumes. This, however, Is not the case, unless all the jackets are made in precisely the same way, which is not very likely to happen. The piqué collars, revers, cuffs, false jackets, and walscoats must always follow the lines of those of the cloth costume; if worn under- neath, they must be a little wider and larger, so as to show a border of white ; if worn over, they must be a little smaller, the cloth or serge revers, etc., providing a dark border to the white pique ornaments. _ _ _

Again the Princetse robe and fanciful skirt has appeared, and one so admirable for promenade and visiting wear that I shall not be astonished if it meets with the approval it most certainly merits. The model I have seen is of dark green diagonal cloth, mounted over Manua-coloured Bengaline, but the colours may be what you will so long as you choose with discretion und blend them with skill.

Simple wraps for children are indispensable for autumn wear. The model of a loose straight coat which has been brought under my notice is not only piquant in style, but easily made at home. Self-coloured cloth with a smooth-faoe is suitable for the model, and any width from forty-six to fifty inches may be used, or less if thin material is used. Two and a-half widths are required for the full skirt portion. If the fronts are cut entire, three double widths are needed. No lining is necessary in the cloak, but a strip of canvas and a facing of silk make it strong and neater, and half a yard of silk is sufficient to face the front from the yoke, if cut in strips, or one yard for yoke and fronts. The shape is suitable for girls of from four to 10 years.

I will describe a few of the charming specimens of novel bonnets submitted to me; two especially won my admiration with their charm of good taste and perfect style. One extremely becoming to a middle-aged matron, was round in shape and had a low crown, the brim curving slightly upwards in the centre of the front, drooped to either side over half-concealbd roses placed squarely in the front. This bonnet was in black beaver, lined with a lovely shade of pale golden-tan felt, the crown encircled with a coronet of very small black ostrich tips upstanding, and a high group at the left Bide; the tie was of broad glace ribbon fastened beneath the chin in a square bow. Another, equally stylish, displayed a decided novelty in the straw- plait of strands of narrow satin and chenille whereof the brim was formed, in over-lapping, pill. like rows, into a most becoming outline, raised in front with a rosette of black satin, and a fan. shaped decoration of little feather tips, at each side of the jet crown terminating in loops of satin ribbon with strings of the slime. A handsome osprey was raised at one side of the front. Another hat was of black felt bound with velvet, and trimmed with black plumes and large paste buckle.

Braiding is in high favour at the moment, and a capital example I have seen has a bodice of red cloth completely coversd with a find hand braid- ing, with a " military curl" of broad,T braid set in the centre in a shape which would prove most becoming to the figure. The e'eaves and skirt of this are of the cloth, and at the hem of the latter appears a nE,rro-x band r=f the red cloth outlined with the braid which takes bare and there the "military curl" again, whiie the former, which are wide and full at th,) top, ate caught in fiat pleats from elbow to wrest with a row of the nar- row braid, and at the waist and neck a black satin ribbon is tries to form a pleasing finish. Yours lovingly, J EN NIE.

THE EXAMINER. SATURDAY, OCTOBER 13, 1894. THE DOUBLE LIFE OR, THE HAMPTON MYSTERY, By the Author of " DAUSWOOD "

Teresita Canzio, the daughter of Garibaldi, is writing a life of her father. _

A Peculiar disease of the will is said to be becoming very common on the Continent. The sufferer is unable to cross any open space, like a city square. The telegraph companies of Australia suffer a great loss every year from the fact that t,he natives have a special fondness for bracelets and earrings made of tele- graph wire. Parrots are put to a practical test in Germany. 'I hey, it is said, have been introduced into the railway stations and trained to call out the name while the trains stands there, and thus save people the trouble of making enquiries. The Dowager Duchess of Newcastle has taken a house near the Roman Catholic Church of the English Martyrs on Tower-hill, where she will be able more effectually to carry en her social work among the poor of the East-end. Her Grace, with other ladies, will take up her residence there shortly. In every apartment used by the Queen, not even ex- cluding the dining-room, there is always an inkstand for her Majesty's convenience, in case of emergency pro- vided with a fresh quill and pen. For ordinary continu- ous correspondence, however, the Queen prefers a steel nib, although the " Victoria R," with the nice little smudge underpeath, is, of course, tbe work of a quill. Madame Tolstoi is a remarkable woman, who received a diploma from the Moscow University at the age of seventeen, was married when she was eighteen, and her husband twenty years older, and is now, after thirty- one years of married life, the mother of nine living children, and her husband's potent aid in his literary labours. Until her children are ten years old she makes all their clothes. She copies and re-copies her husband's manuscript, a task of which the difficulty is increased by the self-invented shorthand in which Count Tolstoi sets down his compositions.

CHAPTER XXXITT. THE TWO SUITORS.

The afternoon was declining on the day subse- quent to that on which occurred Lord Grosvenor's visit to Lord Adlowe. Giralda had wandered away from Trevelyan Park, and from the society of ths fond old marquis, and stood leaning against the fence inclosing the large shrubbery, looking wist- fully up and down the road, with a vague expectancy in her glances. She was pale and troubled and thoughtful. She had' not heard from Lord Gros ienor since his de- parzure, and fears for his safety, mingled with her anxieties for her parents, tortured her soul almost beyond endurance. "Oh, Paul! Paul 1" she murmured. "If I have involved you in the trouble of our family—if I have brought down on your head the hatred of our en shall never forgive myself !" She choked back a sob, and strove hard to main- tain hr calmness.

It was at this juncture that the sound of a hors,)'s hoofs on the stony road caught her attention. She opened the little wicket-gate, and half advanced into the road.

-- Do You Like Bloaters?

"It is Paul !" she rmurnured, her face becoming transfigured with light and joy. "It is surely Paul !"

If so, the way to get the freshest and best is by writ- ing to Messrs. Pleasance and Co., 64, Admiralty Road, Great Yarmouth, who make a speciality of them. The best material only is used in the curing of these bloaters, viz. :—oak billett, which gives them the delicious flavour so common in the olden time. The bloaters themselves can only be said to be in their prime from September to the middle of December. Yarmouth is famed, not only for its bloaters, but for its herrings; both commodities being sent out in boxes, at a low price, to all parts of the country.

She drew back a little into the shadow, nearly closing the gate, until she was assured beyond the possibility of a doubt that the advancing horseman was no other than her young lover. Then she shyly opened the gate again, and stood revealed to the new-comer, blushing and half con- fused. with great glowing eyes looking like wells of love.

Lord Grosvenor saw her, galloped towards her, and hastily dismounted, taking her trembling bands in both h.s own.

The "Flue." Influenza is a disease which occurs suddenly, and assumes the form of .an epidemic. It rarely stays in any particular town or city for more than a few weeks, and then passes on to fresh scenes. A recent epidemic is supposed to have passed from Chinese Tartary to Russia, Germany, Holland, England, Scotland, and finally America. What it is due to, or where it originates, or why it travels about in the eccentric manner, is says " Cassell's Book of the 'Household " not known. It is not a question of soil, for it attacks those who live on the hills just as frequently as those who dwell on the plains. It has no particular connection with the season of the year, and does not depend in any way on variations in temperature. It seems to have little to do with the prevalence of fogs or mists, or with barometric pressure, or the amount of ozone in the air. It attacks not only men, women, and children, but also dogs, horses, and cats. It generally begins with the symptoms of an ord- inary cold in the head, and if the patient is not on the alert he may not suspect the real nature of his attack. In a few hours, however, he begins to experience an in- crecible sense of languor and weakness, which quickly incapacitates him from exertion of all kinds. He not only cannot get about, but he feels as if he could hardly lift a finger. He lies on the sofa during the day, goes to bed early, and gets up very late. His skin is hot and dry, tha pulse is quick,there i§ some cough and wheezing about the chest, and there may be persistent vomiting or diarrhcea. This condition lasts a week or more ; and even when the symptoms of the acute attack have passed off, the prostration is still very great, and may not be entirely recovered from for several months. Bronchitis--or, at all events, bronchial cataarh—is not at all an uncommon accompaniment, and this naturally increases the gravity of the illness.

He looked so b.ight and joyous at seeing her that Gin.lda's heart leaped, and she forgot for a moment ad her griefs and anxieties. She let her hands remain in his close, warm clasp, she let her head droop to his breast, and she listened in a sort of delicious trance to the Words of love and tender- ness that sprang impulsively from his lips and heart.

Suddenly she started, her anxieties returcing in full force.

"You have been to The Evergreens, Paul ?" she said, lifting her head. "You have warned my father?"

"Yes, Giralda. I made all possible haste, and arrived at The Evergreens in the evening. Your mailer was there. Lord Adlowe had just de- parted from the house, and had seen your parents together, and had penetrated the disguise of your • father."

Giralda uttered a low, startled cry, and trembled as with an ague. "My father is lost, then 1" she moaned. "He is lost !''

"Not' so, Criralda," said her lover, tenderly. "We acted promptly. We sent your father away immediately in the pony-carriage to catch the ex- press train at a distant station, and at this moment he is, with Rupert, at a small house of mine in a secluded region where his presence will excite no remark, and where it will puzzle even Lord Ad- lowe's detective to discover him."

'

Giralda breathed m.)re freely. "Thank Heaven, and you, Paul !" she murmured, under her breath. "And my mother ?" "I escorted her back to London yesterday morning. She is at Hampton House. I told her of my love for you, Giralda, and she has allowed me to assist her in her trials. Poor Lady Bea- trice ! She has need of true and trustworthy friends."

"Poor mamma !" sighed Giralda. "Her troubles are all the harder that they must be borne in se- cret."

"-She will go into the country this week," said Lord Grosvenor. "She has been sorely tried of late, and can scarcely bear the noise of the town." _ _ _

"-And Fay?" questioned Giralda. "You have not mentioned him, Paul. Does he stay alone at The Evergreens—dear little Fay?" The young lord hesitated. He could not bear to pierce this tender, loving soul with the ill news he had brought. He had intended to keep from her the fact of Fay's disappearance, but when she raised to his her wondrous blue eyes, so honest, fearless, and truthful, and seemed to look into his very soul, he could not do otherwise than tell her.

"Be brave, Giralda," he said, gathering her closer to his breast. "When Lord Adlowe went away from The Evergreens, he took Fay with II Giralda repeated the words, as if unable to com- prehend their meaning. Then, as their full import burst upon her, she reeled, and would have fallen but for his lordship's tender support.

"Lord Adlowe has taken Fay away !" she said, as if dazed and bewildered. Poor little Fay! What does he want of Fay, Paul? What will he do with him ?" "You know that Adlowe stands in a critical place," said Lord Grosvenor. "He has discovered at last that he is not likely to inherit the Trevelyan estates, for which he has schemed all his life. He has discovered that his love for the Lady Beatrice Hampton was all worse than wasted. He has expensive tastes, without the means to gratify them. He is too much a stranger in official circles to seek an appointment or a foreign mission, even if his abilities fitted him for such a position. In short, Giralda, his life has been misspent and idled away in foolish pleasures; and now, at forty, he has nothing in himself, nothing of his own! His only hope is to marry a rich heiress. And i that heiress he has found n you, darling, while your beauty, your innocence, and your intelligence have charmed him. He has turned from the Lady Beatrice to you, and I believe that he loves you as much as he is capable of loving. His love for the Lady Beatrice has turned to hatred. You refused him, and I do not doubt that he expects to compel you to marry him, through your love and pity for your little brother !" "Do you think he will harm Fay ?" asked Gir- alda, in a hushed voice. "I do not mean that he shall get the chance," replied Lord Grosvenor cheerfully. "I shall have him watched, and we shall, no doubt, easily track out the little fellow. Have no anxieties, Giralda. All will soon be well !" His bright confidence went far to reassure Gir- alda. She had great faith in his ability to rescue Fay, remembering how he had twice rescued her- self from perils even worse than that threatening her brother. "I will not be greatly troubled, Paul, since you have undertaken his rescue," she said. "But poor mamma! He is her darling—her youngest child 1 I ought to go to her, to comfort her—" "Your best place is here, Giralda," declared her lover, as she paused, with a sudden but st of tears. a Providence sent you here to soften tnat old man's heart to his wronged nephew. Since I have seen your father, Giralda, my confidence in his in- noCence of that terrible charge has become a posi- tive certainty! lie is a grand man, darling—a man incapable of crime. I love him already. How could Lord Trevelyan credit such a charge against him? The circumstantial evidence must have been black indeed How the marqu;s must have loved him, to hate him as he does now !"

"Re did love him as even fathers seldom love !" said Giralda. " I think sometimes that, under all his wrath and bitterness and hatred, he loves him yet. If I can serve mother better here, I will stay, Uncle Trevelyan loves me, and can scarcely bear T-ne to leave his sight. And I love him, Paul. How I wish that papa am:l mamma, and Rupert and Fay, were all here, to share his afrection and to brighten his last days. He is noble and tender, despite all his faults. Paul. I fear that there will never be any reconciliation between papa and his uncle," she added, sorrowfully. "If my uncle were to die, and I sear he will in one of his awful fits of rage, and papa wer, to come forward to succeed him, people would believe that old charge, and say that papa had been in hiding; all tnese years. That would be worse than now, Pagl. Papa's ,Jan e could never be cleared then."

THE BRENNAN TORPEDO.

Mr. Brennan, the inventor of the torpedo, began life as a watchmaker in Australia, and at once developed a genius for invention. from first to last this torpedo, which has proved so profitable to him, cost him eighteen years of hard work. The torpedo was sold to the British Government for one hundred and fifty thousand pounds and to insure the secret of the mechanism being kept, each portion of this engine of destruction is made in a different shop, the workmen are searched on entering; and leaving the building, and Mr. Brennan and his part- ner are the only persons who fix the torpedo together ready for working.

WHERE THEY FEAR NO CO AL STRIKE.

The district of B tku, on the western shore of the Caspian Sea, is says" Little Folks," so rich in oil that the natives make use of it for all sorts of househ,,ld pur- poses. If a small cane, whose top is c wered with clay to, protect it from catching fire, be stuck into the ground for a few inches, and a light be applied, a flame of natural gas will at once issue. In many of the huts, which are merely built upon the bare earth. this is the usual means of obtaining both light and heat. A kettle or pot on a stand, placed over two or three of these cane Sets, will soon boil water or cook food. It need hardly be said that Baku is the centre of a large trade in naphtha.

SHOCKING ATTACK UPON A. LADY.

Tha story of a shocking crime comes from Achill Island. About three miles from Dugort, in Blacksod Bay, Mrs. M'Donnell, said to be the wife of a London barrister, lived alone in what is described as a "county mansion " known as Achill Valley House, and formerly occupied by the Earl of Caven. She is also the owner of a small estate in the district. On Saturday night her house was set on fire by an incendiary, and Mrs. M`Donnell in trying to make her escape was attacked, according to her statement,by a man named Lynchehan, who was, or formerly had been, her agent, but with whom some unpleasantness had arisen. She was shame- fully maltreated, her skull being beaten in by a heavy stone, and she states that Lynchehan attempted to throw her into the flames. Mrs. M‘Donnell is not ex- pected to recover. Her supposed assailant has been arrested.

"I must go !" Paul exclaimed at last, marking the fading sunset. "I will not go in to see Lord Trevelyan, as I am only on my way home from Trevelyan stition. I intended to see you, how- ever' Giralda, before going home. I shall go back to London by the morning train, and shall call at the Park as I go. One word more, darling. I thought that Lord Adlowe, all muffled, was on the train, but when I looked more narrowly for him I could not find him. Be careful of yourself. He may mean you harm." Giralda promised, and Lord Grosvenor walked with her through the shrubbery, pausing in the shadow of the trees fringing the lawn, to say good- by. There was a tender lingering embrace, a soft shower of kisses on the girl's sweet face, a last word, and then the young lord tore himself away, hastening back to his house. Giralda stood where he had left her until the ring of hoofs told her that he had indeed departed, and then she turned back into the shrubbery, unwilling to trust her blushing cheeks and shining eyes under the obeservation of the keen-eyed marquis. She walked along a narrow path full of twilght shadows, and sat down on a rustic bench encircling a large pine. She had scarcely done so when a figure stalked out of the neighbouring gloom—the figure of Lord Axllowe !

Giralda sprang up at sight of him with a stifled cry—he looked so dark and fierce and desperate. "Sit down 1" he said, hoarsely, waving her back to the bench. "I have seen the Count and Countess of Arevalo face to face—have visited them at their suburban retreat, The Evergreens. I have discovered that the Spanish Count' is no other than Geoffrey Trevelyan in disguise, and that you Miss Giralda, as I suspected, are really and truly the grandniece of the doting old marquis, whom you have so cleverly befooled." Giralda made no reply to this speech, but shrank back, her eyes dilating with iinreasing terror.

"Ah I see that you are already informed of Any visit to your parents I" exclaimed Adlowe. "Did Lord Grosvenor, whom I saw on the train, also tell you that I have assumed the charge of your little brother' and that Fay is safely hidden where he will not be found until I choose to give him up?" Giralda's pale face and terrified eyes answered for her better than words could have done. "Then," said Adlowe, roughly, "you can thoroughly comprehend the situation, Miss Trevel- yan. The Marquis may die, and you will inherit his spare money, while your father will claim the title and entailed property. Cleverly planned! But so long as little Fay is missing your cup of *joy will have an infusion of bitterness. All the gold and honours will not bring balm to the heart of the Lady Beatrice—" "Oh, Lord Adlowe 1" interrupted Giralda, in a whisper. "What are you going to do with Fay? Do you want money for him ?" "Yes, and a wife !" said Adlowe, grimly. "I have wasted my best years in a sentimental devo- tion to the Lady Beatrice Hampton. Her daughter must recompense me. On the day that you be- come my Wife little Fay shall be restored to his mother.'

" Oh ! I cannot! I cannot !" exclaimed Giralda, wringing her hands. "But you shall !" cried Adlowe, his eyes gleam- ing fierily. "I am not to be balked again by a feeble girl! lam a desperate man! Defeated in my expectations of heirship—made a mock of by the lady I loved—a poor, penniless adventurer, after all my grand schemes—l will not endure it! I will retrieve myself! You, Miss Giralda, shall be my stepping-stone to wealth. Refuse me'again at your peril ! He hissed the last sentence, bestowing upon her the look of an infuriated demon.

Giralda thought of flight, but her limbs were for the moment paralyzed. She strove to command her thoughts, but chaos reigned in her soul. "I have taken my precautions well this time," said her enemy, and a serpent's cunning gleamed in his eyes. You escaped from the bluff cottage. You will never escape me again! I have too much at stake to risk another defeat. Give me your solemn promise to marry me, and I will permit you to return to the house; refuse me, and I shag take you away with me. Ihave a wagon waiting near for the purpose." "Not even to save my nearest and dearest ones can I marry you, Lord Adlowe !" cried Giralda, finding voice at last. "Heaven have mercy upon my persecuted family, since you will not !" "You refuse me, then ?" and Adlowe's eyes blazed with fury. "I warned you! Your fate be on your own head !" • He sprang toward her with a leap like a tiger. Giralda uttered a wild shriek of utter terror, and at that moment the bushes parted, and the old Marquis Trevelyan rushed into the cleared space and paused, looking from one to the other of the pair with surprise, scorn, hatred and inquiry. mingled in his gaze. A POLITE " HELP."—A lady hired a Western country girl for a family "help," and was sur- prised to see her poke her head into the parlor one afternoon when visitors were present, and ask, "Marm, did you call just now? I thought 1 heard a yell."

DESAVING THE BASTE."—"I engaged," says a tourist, "a chaise at Galway to conduct me some few miles into the country, and had not proceeded far when it pulled up at the foot of a hill, and the Irish driver coming to the door, opened it. 'What are you at, man? This isn't where I ordered you to stop." Whisht, your honor! whisht ! 'said Paddy in an under-tone; I am only desaving the baste. I'll just burg the door; he'll think yer out; and then he'll cut up the hill like Old Scratch. See if he don't !' "

THE APOSTLE OF THE INDIANS.—John Eliot, the apostle of the Indians, sought his home in 1631 on the comparatively barren shores of New England, and then plunged into the dark forests in which the American Indians dwelt, to carry them the message of the Gospel. It took him twelve years to master there language, but with untiring energy he applied himself to his task, and succeeded in completing a translation of the Scriptures into their tongue. He was not preacher only, but the great social leader and civiliser of the Indians, and his power of conquering difficulties and carrying to successful issue whatever he undertook, even when opposed by overwhelming odds, entitles him to universal admiration. His was no spasmodic zeal, but a downright earnest love for the men whose interest he had at heart, and not until he had laboured among the Indians for fifty years did he relax his labours, and then only because he was no longer able to work. Half a century later, David Brainerd, inspired with a like zeal, buried himself in the wilderness, a solitary white man among wild tribes whose most coveted trophies were the scalps of their victims; dwelling now in a log hut, and now wandering through the forests and sleeping in the open air by a pinewood fire, kindled not only to keep off the damp, but to scare away the wolves that prowled around his bed; then up and away to tell in some wigwam the story of the Cross, or to arrest the progress of a savage tracking the steps of some beast of chase, with words of prayer. At length another fire than that of Christian zeal was burning in the young missionary. The intolerable fatigues, the night damps, and ex- cessive vigils had done their work on his tender frame, and at the age of twenty-nine he died of consumption, cheered by the knowledge that there were six other men ready to take up the work as he laid it down. _ _ _ _

A CASE FOR M. ZOLA.

The great French realist has lately brought into prominence his views on the Lourdes Miracles. M. Zola appears to say that all these cures are explicable on purely scientific grounds. He, then, would no doubt fully endorse the following " miracle," which is directly traced to scientific causes. A lady crippled and totally incapacitated for over eight years, and given over by the best medical skill as utterly hopeless, discarded her crutches, and recovered her health, under circumstances so striking that the " Midland Free Press" made a full enquiry as to the genuineness of the case. The story is v,auched for in every particular, and has been verifie.d by the most unsparing independent investigation. The patient (for this unlike most miracles, admits of chapter- and-verse verification) was Mrs. Cardwell, 6 Avenue- buildings, Cavendish-road, Aylestone-park, Leicester " Eight years ago last Christmas, I was compelled to go with crutches," she said " I couldn't use my legs at all I was in Leicester Infirmary nearly five years ago, was an out-patient afterwards for eight months, and an in- patient at Bristol Infirmary Hospital, also." She was in a perfectly helpless state, until one day she was induced to try the effect of Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People. That was about eight months ago, and the results have been aptly described as miraculous. She in:Tr:Ned from the first, and the paralysis in her legs be- gan to pass away. "I never wore a shoe for years," she said, "and now I can sit at my ease or move about freely. I was never warm but now I am comfortabla and well. My health is better every way. NEW BOOKS, MAGAZINES, ETC.

MACMILLAN AND Co.—From these publishers we have another of the Cranford Series, viz.— " Coridon's Song and other verses," with illustra- tions from the pencil of Hugh Thompson. This last statement should be quite sufficient to draw all lovers of art into the meshes of a speculation for the purchase of " Coridon's Song, etc " A characteristic introduction by Mr. Austin Dobson gives an account of the various poems illustrated in this volume, and brings out incidentally much of the charm which belongs not only to them, but to their period. The volume is beauti- fully bound and can be had for the moderate sum of 63. —A new and complete edition of Lord Tennyson's works is just published. All the poems which the poet laur- . _ . .

eate wrote are embodied in this volume, inclusive of his last, "Crossing the Bar," which is so t3uchingly sug- gestive of his being conscious of "One clear call for me." Demeter, and the death of /none, are also included in the list of additions since the previous publication. An excellent vignetted engraving of the poet prefaces the book which is bound in green cloth and printed in light, clear, type on good paper, (price 7s. 6d.). We are also in receipt of "The Use of Life," by the right hon. Sir John Lubbock, Bart., M.P., who, as a standard writer will, no doubt, command the attention of every thinking man and woman. The volume abounds in apt quota- tions and proverbs, each one illustrating the author's ideas and sentiments. The opening chapters deal with the great question of "how to live," and in the first sentence he says "there is nothing men are so anxious to keep as life, and nothing they take so little pains to keep well." Religion, education, social life, money matters, and tact are some of the heads of his discourse, as it were, the whole commanding itself to our careful study and perusal. Valuable as the book is from a liter- ary point of view, it is cheap enough to bring it within the reach of all thoughtful readers. CASSELL AND CO.—The first part of a new volume of "Chums" is to hand and is in no way inferior to the

preceding publication. A great hit has now been made by the editor, who has secured the right to publish—of all things in the world—that wonderful story of Robert Louis Stevenson—" Treasure Island," of which in this number appear the first two chapters, with a capital picture of the well-remembered scene in which " The Captain," with drawn cutlass, is pursuing" Black Deg " from the doorway of the Admiral Benbow Inn, while the landlord's son, from whose pen this marvellous story is supposed to proceed, peeps out aghast upon the scene from behind the inn door. If " Chums " had wanted anything to make it go like wild-fire, this might have been thought enough. But there is also a new serial tale by George Manville Fenn, "The Queen's Scarlet, or 'By Your Right ' " ; a thrilling story of a mad lighthouse- k pper, entitled "In Direst Peril " ; an interview by Lord Montmorres with Sir John Millais, who has some kindly words for boys who would like to be artists; a " Ride on the Flying Dutchman " ; "All about Robert Louis Stevenson ." and his life in Samoa; and a host of adven- turous or funny stories, experiments, puzzles, prize competitions, and interesting tit-bits of all sorts, that crowd all the odd corners of these sixteen broad attrac- tive pages. A fine tinted picture is presented with this number, reproduced from George Joy's famous picture of "Wellington's First Encounter with the French" - also a stirring picture in colours as frontispiece, entitled "In the Enemy's Grip," illustrating an incident in Indian warfare. It may confidently be expected that when this anhouncement is seen and made known, all romance and story-loving boys will rush to the nearest stationers to procure a copy.—This month also sees the issue of Part 1 of Cassell's " New Popular Educator," price 6d." The comprehensive and enlarged basis en Iv hich this revised edition is reconstructed will enable it to accomplish as effectively for the present generation that which as testified by the most eminent, scholastic and philanthropic authorities, it has in its original form achieved for many thousands of our countrymen." In history, geography, science, art, languages and literature the " Popular Educator" will be found to be invaluable to the student. When the volume is completed it will contain 48 plates and maps printed in colours, together with numerous illustrations.—The" Universal Atlas," a new and complete general atlas of the world, to contain 117 pages of maps handsomely printed in colours, and a complete index with about 125,000 names is now com- mencing its first volume. Part 1 contains a double-page general map of France and a single-page one of Egypt. A good atlas is an indispensable requisite in every , home. It will therefore be acceptable news to the British public to learn that this magnificent atlas is to be issued in a form that will place it within the reach of all, viz.: 6d. per month. F. BLACKBERRYING AT APPLETON OR THE UNDOING OF JONES.

(BY VERNON HAYES).

Most people have their weaknesses, some in one way, some in another. As a lad my own weakness lay chiefly in my knees, which same weakness proved rather an expensive one in the way of trousers as, through rubbing together, they soon wore out. Singularly enough, my present day weakness is productive of similar results, but in quite a different way. Let me explain. lam par- ticularly fond of blackberry jelly. "Well, and what has blackberry jelly got to do with trousers ?" you may ask. I answer a good deal,' that is, if you gather the blackberries yourself; and I always like to do that. True, one may buy the berries in the market ready to hand, and so save the trousers,but then there are blackberries and—well, berries of other hues, and it always seems to me that the latter most blushingly preponderate in a quart of the bought variety. Consequently, I like to gather them for myself even though, as I said before, it does come expensive in the matter of trousers. Last season I completely ruined one pair through tramp- ing in the wet grass of the fields, to say nothing of the boots that were spoiled in addition. Being in no way daunted by the remembrance of these disasters however, I was 'quite ready for another expedition this year, and so when about a fortnight ago my wife gently broke the news to me that the last pot of our last season's 'boiling of jelly was the one upon which we were then engaged, I felt that the time was drawing near for me to sally fourth to do battle with the brambles (not to mention the stinging nettles) for the sake of the shining luscious fruit. Thursday last, being half holiday,l.fixed as the date of attack, and after surrounding a good dinner (an important point on these excursions, as one Bever knows what time may elapse before partaking of the next meal) I hied me to the kitchen and selected the biggest can I could find, then going to the hat-stand I picked out the stick I always cake with me on these ,occasions. It is a blackthorn and formerly belonged to my old grandad. I believe he brought it over with him from Ireland when he first came tcesettle in these parts. By the way I've often wondered why it is that I so invariably take this particular stick with me on my blackberrying expeditions for I never think of taking it out at any other time. In fact, I don't suppose I ever notice its presence in the stand.; but so surely as I go to select a stick for blackberrying, so surely is this blackthorn the one upon swhich my eyes and hand involuntarily rest. There is evidently some psychological mystery connected with it which I must make it my duty to inquire about. Thus -armed and provided for-the fray, there arose the question—' W here should I gd?' Hill Cliff, the Dingle, and one or two other local haunts suggested themselves, but having for day, aye for weeks past, met groups of children going out in the direction of these places with the tell-tale cans in their hands, I speedily concluded that to be successful I had better try and pastures new. A few moments consideration decided me upon trying the Arley neighbourhood for a change. I set out, and going by the Lumb Brook road, an hour's good walking brought me to Appleton Thorn. Close by the church I chanced to!meet an acquaint- ance from town, who looked as warm and perspiring as I did myself. I remarked, "It's a hot day " ! ,'Tes," said he, "it's very close." "Indeed," I said, "whereto?" He looked at me more in sorrow than anger and replied slowly "To the Thorn,' and as you are -so funny, you may pay for two drinks." That was the reward for my little .joke. However, we promptly adjourned to the " Th orn" for a liquid refresher. Whilst sitting there in the cool and comfortable parlour, quaffing our ale, I was seized with a sudden misgiving. Was Zones (by-the-way, pardon me, I forgot to introduce you before) was Jones out on a similar expedition'? I had come a long way to have the country to myself and my object would be defeated if Jones had come equally far for the same reason and on the same errand. True, so far as I could bee he had no can or basket with him, but then he might possibly have some patent collapsible affair concealed somewhere about his person, .purposely contrived to mislead people as to his intentions. Better get to`know his object at once I thought, so here goes. "What are you doing out this way to-day Jones?" •" Oh, nothing in particular. .Just come round this way for a ramble." That sounded innocent enough but I thought I had better be quite sure about him. "Did you happen to see any blackberries as you came along?" I asked. "Blackberries?" Be seemed to ponder a moment or two, as though he'd never heard of such things before. "Blackberries? No, ! I can't say that I noticed any," ("good business," thought I, "then he's not on my lay,") •"But why do you ask?" " Because that's what I'm after," I said, and I showed him the can which until then had borne the appearance of a respectable brown paper parcel. Continuing, "Look here, Jones," I said, "as you don't appear to have any particular business on hand, suppose you come along with me and help me to gather them. You'll enjoy it immensely." And I launched forth into a glowing account of the delights of blackberrying, and with such good effect that he soon became as enthused , with the idea as I was myself. We quickly drank up and departed on ear quest. About half-an-hour's walk from the 'Thorn'. brought us to a field, close to the gate of which stood a post with a board beating the time-hon- oured legend Trespaseere will he prosecuted.' Now whenever I see this notice I always make a point of trespassing and I feel somewhat aggrieved that so far no one on whose land I have trespassed has taken the least notice and as I think I have a right to be prosecuted PS well as anybody else I feel almost inclined to sue those responsible for the notices for breach of contract and damages. But to continue. We could see a goodly array of brambles on the inside hedges of the field, and notwithstand- ing the warning on the board, we climbed the gate and were about to commence the business on hand when it occurred to me that perhaps it might be as :well, in view of the warning, that one of us should "keep nicks " as the lads say, whilst the other scoured the hedge for berries, for although, as you will have understood by my previous remarks, I don't mind taking the risk of prosecution myself, yet I thought it would scarcely be right to let Jones in as well. So I mentioned it to him and he agreed with me. Tnen we tossed for the division of labour. "Heads or tails, Jones, for choice ?" " Heads !" " Heads it is so which will you clO With all the enthusiasm of a new hand he chose to do the gathering, so that I had to keep watch at the gate. I unwrapped the can, and giving it to Jones, he started off whilst I climbed on to the top bar of the gate so as to command a good view of anyone coming. Taking out my pipe and tobacco, I lit UP and prepared to enjoy myself. Jones seemed to be doing fairly well amongst the berries, calling out encouraging reports every now and then. The field was of an irregular shape. and soon Jones had worked his way round a corner out of my sight. Presently my enforced idleness began to pall, and happening to have a copy of Tit-Bits in my pocket I drew it out and began to read, throw- ing occasional glances around to see that we were not interrupted. I had been reading for about a quarter of an hour when I was startled by a yell from Jones which made the welkin ring. Hastily jumping down from my perch, I ran as quickly as I could round the corner where I had last seen him. Arrived there, I stared blankly about me. It re- minded one of the puzzles that some of our trades- people send out—" The fields, the brambles, and Jones—Puzzle, find Jones !" for indeed he was no- where to be seen. "Where the dickens has he•—•" " HELP ! HELP !!" came again. I ran forward in the direction from which the sound came and presently espied Jones, or rather his head, for that was all that was visible of him, looking like a veritable Father Neptune and covered with weeds and green slime. He had fallen into a pit. He looked so ridiculous that in spite of his dangerous position I burst out laughing. However, quickly realizing the untimeliness of my mirth I got to work to lie:p him. Making my way carefully down to the edge of the pond, I held out my blackthorn to him as far as I could reach and with an effort he managed to grasp the other end, and I gradually drew him ashore. When he gained the bank, he was a most pitiable object, covered with a mantle of green slime and mud. As he stood there spluttering the dirty water out of his mouth he looked so forlornly damp and miserable that I again burst out laughing at his sorry figure. " What are you laughing at, you confounded idiot?" he yelled. " Isn't it bad enough for me to have fallen into that filthy hole without having you laughing and grinning at me like a Cheshire cat !" I managed to control myself sufficiently to be able to ask him how be had managed to tumble into such a place. " Why its all along of you and your confounded black- berries" (I thought it was rather nasty of Jones

===== 0002601_18941013_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
to say that when he had chosen to do the gathering part himself) "I was going along the hedge bottom" he continued, " not thinking of looking behind me. when suddentiv I felt my feet slip and before I knew what had happened I was head over heels in the water, I came up once and yelled and then sank again, on coming up the second time I yelled for all I was worth." "Well, what's to be done now r I sail," for it'll never do for you to walk home in those wet clothes. It would be the death of you." " Well, what do you suggest ?" he asked, " since it's all through you I've got into this pickle. I shall expect you to see me out of it." " Certainly, you may rely upon me doing what I can," I replied, and looking round I saw at a little distence away a small cottage," The very thing," I cried, "look here, Jones, old man you must go into the pond again." " Not he replied with a considerable degree of warmth," I'm qaite wet enough as it is, thank you." "Oh, not with your clothes on—l don't mean that,you must strip yourself and swim about a bit while I go to yonder cottage and get your clothes dried and perhaps I may get them to lend me a suit for you to wear un- til your own are ready." After some little demur he agreed to this proposal and stripped off his clothes and plunged in the pond again., whilst I set off carrying the clothes towards the cottage. To get there, it was necessary to go out by the gate, and I had just got my bead 'round the corner of the field when, horror upon horror, who should I see but the farmer. tie 'had evidently just come in the field, and was fastening the gate behind him. As his back was turned, ofcourse he didn't see me, and I bolted o'ff badk -again to tell Jones of this new dilemma. " Great Scott !" he cried when I'd told him "this is worse and worse. What shall we do now .?" " -Goodness knows" I re- plied, "I might make a bolt for it, but you—werl you could scarcely tear your way through these hedges with nothing 0n.." "Tell you 'what," said Jones after a pause suppose you getinto the pit along with me. We could -duck ourtends under the water until he gets by:" 6` No,thanks," I replied, politely but very firmly, "I'm not taking any to- day. Besides, your clothes on the tank would give us away at once." "There seemed to'he nothing for it but to stand our groulid, although 'that expres- sion scarcely fits Jones's case, as he was treading water. In a minute or two, which seemed an age to us, the farmer rounded the corner, and on catch- ing sight of me, he shouted out" Ili rthere what are you doing inthis field. Don't you know you're trespassing?" I beckoned him to come up and when he had got within hail, I commenced—" Look here farmer!, we know we've 'been trespassing, but per- haps you'll overlook it this time, seeing how unfor- tunate my friend has been," and' here I pointed to Jones, whose head was bobbing up and down.in the water like an animated cork 6 'float. The farmer looked in the direction I 'indicated and when he caught sight of Jones—" Why, what on earth's he doing there?'" he exclaimed, looking quite bewildered, as well he 'might, to see a man apparently having a bathe 'in a stagnant pond Thereupon I explained the whole circumstances and shewed him ifone's dripping clothes. And then how that farmer did laugh. 'Upon my word I thought he'd have choked, and there was Jones all the while looking -reproachfully at me as though I could help it. Whennt last the farmer Was able to speak again-4' Well, well." he said, "your friend had better getout and come home with me as quick as he can and we'll dry his things for him. As for the trespassing, we'll say :no more about that, he's had enough punishment already." Thanking the farmer leartily for his kindness I quickly helped Janes 044 t again and we all marched off; the farmer and I in 'front and Jones behind arrayed in a macintosh which the farmer fortun- ately had with 'him. 'We managed to reach the farm by a quiet and unfrequented road without meeting anyone. Arrived there the farmer lent Jones a suit of clothes to wear whilst his own were being dried. Nordid his kindness end at that for we soon afterwards sat down to a jolly good ten. followed by a pipe and a glass Of hot grog for Jones to ward off the possible ill-effects of his immersion, In a little while it was announced that Jones's clothes were ready, and whenhe had chant-A we again expressed OUT gratitude to the farmer for his kindness and then started on our homeward way. We had no blaCkberries to carry however,for both the can and its contents had joined Jones in his in- voluntary aquatic display, and ef,course their share in the performance 'ended when they reached the bottom of the pond. We arrived in town soon after dark and parted company to go-to our respective homes. I got in a TOW for losing the can, but I haven't yet heard what 'Jones's wife snid to him, though I'm told she wouldlike to have a few words with me.

P.S. About that blackthorn. Since writing the above I have made searching inquiries into the matter with a result which must prove particularly interesting to believers in the theory of heredity. It seems that my grandfather always took this partic- ular stick with him on the occasion and only then) of his " going to a &trying "1

MR. GLADSTONE'S GitA NDFATHER.

Mr. Gladstone has replied to a correspondent, who drew his attention to a statement in an Edinburgh news- paper that Mr. Gladstone's grandfather was a baker in Leith during the Bread Riots, and was held in such detestation by the mob that on several occasions he sought safety in precipitate flight, and would have had his earthly career abruptly terminated had he remained in Leith. Mr. Gladstone says his grandfather was not a baker, so far as he knows,was uniyersaLly respectcd,and resided in Leith to the last.

IHE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, OCTOBER 13' 1894

THE RUNCORN TECHNICAL

INSTITUTE.

MR. BRUNNER AND HIS SECOND PROMISE. Mr. F. Wiswall presided over a meeting of the General (Hybrid) Committee of the Technical Insti- tute on Monday evening in the Board-room of the Town Hall, Runcorn. There were also present the Rev. Conon Wood, Rev. Dr. Gastaldi,and Messrs. S. Taylor, J.P., H. J. Grice, J. B. Holland, W. Hol- land, W. E. Dudley, and E. Marshall, secretary. The Cliairman said they had raised £5OO since the last appeal and had applied to Mr. Brunner, M.P., for his original promise of £5OO, in reply to which he had forwarded a cheque for that amount. Their liability had reduced from £1,338 to £l.llO. Those working people who had been approached had said that the insti- tute would not be for them but for the children of the middle classes. It was a pity the word " class" had been introduced, and he did not like to use it ; but he feared that if the working class did not support the institute by their subscriptions it would become an insti- tution for the middle class, because the fees would thereby have to be raised. It would be their own fault. They were trying to open the building free from debt, and so have the £4OO at their disposal for maintenance. If they had to borrow the money to clear off the debt on the building, the interest thereon would have to come out of the sum collected from the rates and the grant of the County Council. He afterwards produced and read the following letter from Mr. Brunner, M.P., dated October 6th :—" I read your latest appeal for subscrip- tions with much pleasure, and am now glad to take this opportunity of expressing my cordial agreement with the belief you entertain that in after years the inhabitants of Runcorn who have helped in the establishment of your Technical Institute, will be remembered with gratitude and respect. It has been suggested to me that if I Offered my second £5OO in instalments of £lOO eachtnmeet each £lOO as subscribed, I should strengthen your hands. If you are also of this opinion and also your committee, you are at liberty to announce that this arrangement has been made with me. Heartily wishing you success, a success you thoroughly deserve, I remain yours faithfully, John T. Brunner.— To Francis Wiserall'Esq." Several suggestions were:made by which subscriptions might be obtained and the meeting afterwards closed.

MELANCHOLY END OF A RUNCORN PITBLICAN'S WIFE.

THE INQUEST AND FUNERAL.

On Monday afternoon at the Runcorn Cemetery Offices, Mr. J. E. Worslev, coroner, and a jury, the foreman of which was Mr. William Wright,Church- st., investigated the circumstances attending the sad death of Agnes Anderton, a portion of whose body was found in number two lock of the Bridge- water Canal on the previous Saturday morning. Owing to the deceasad lady being a native of the town, and therefore well-known, her sudden and mysterious disappearance on the 27th September gave rise to the wildest rumours. Speculation as to her probable fate was very rife, many reasons were spoken of as being the cause of her sudden disappearance. A large crowd of persons t dllected outside the‘Cemetery Offices during the progress of the inquest, evidently with the view to gleaning any further information of the unfortunate woman's decease, and to await the jury's verdict. The 'first witness was William Anderton, landlord of the Anchor ;Inn, Brunswick-st., and husband 'of the deceased. He said that his wife was 41 years of.aee and had been rather weak in health for a long time. He always noticed that she was much worse when she took drink. In•fact when she was drunk she was quite a different woman. They were always happy when -she was sober. 'He had been married to her nearly eighteen years. .oflate she seemed to have become mudh weaker and more inluenced by drink. It was a mystery to him where she got the drink to get intoxicated as for the I last five 'years she had never been allowed to serve'beerl behind the bar. He had had a special door made so that he could not get into the bar, and had hiddetrthe keys of all places from whence the drink could be 'pro- cured. About four years ago a person tame into the house and said that she (deceased) would 'have drowned herself if she had not been prevented by neighbours. She had not been like that until some two or three weeks previously. Within the past three weeks-she seemed to have thought a great deal of her 'late father, and had spoken a his having been drowned at Astmoor. On more than one occasion when still thinking of her father she had-said, "You will find me like that some of these days." She had gone away from 'home and re- mained away for weeks together, but the .morning After she had gone he had always known to where she had gone. he left' home a week last Thursday and before leaving she came • through the vault door whilst witness was at his tea. She said to the daughter Edith "Give merny silver spoons," and the daughter asked•witness she should give them to her mother. Witness replied to her in the affirmative and the spoons were •given. His , wife thenreew out in a temper without giving him an I answer to somethitig he had said to her. That wasthe last time 'he saw her alive. She had nothing to put her i out of temper before that. She had been with her aunt, who was a teetotaler at Southport, with a view tocure', her of intemperance. she remained there a month. and 1 the aunt had written to him saying that his wife was getting clear of drink. About a month previously he returned from Southport and everything waefvery nice for a short time. Before she had gone she had been getting drunk but he did not upbraid her 'before she went. The seoend day after coming back she was 'very bad and seemed as though she could not recover .herself. She became very excitable and said she could not help it. She had very strange ways and would sleep with the children for a month at a time. On the I hursday morning, the same day on which she went away,he went to Altrincham Agricultural :Show and was told when he came back that she had gore to bed at two O'clock, got up at 5.30, and had gone out shortly afterwards. When she went outthe last time he saw her, she was mot eober. Thera was no question as to the body in the mortuary being that of his wife. Edith Anderton, daughter of tie deceased, said that the reason her mother went to Southport was to see if she could recover from her drinkieg habits. After corn- ing back she again took to drink until the day on which she disappeared. On 'the Thursday afternoon she had had some drink, but witness did not notice at night if

her mother was sober. After supporting her father's statement with regard to the spoons, she said that it would be about 7 p.m. when her mother left the house for the last time. Her father waskind to her mother when shelwas sober.and he was kind to her when she was drunk, He used to let her have drink sometimes when she had had some, thinking it would pacify her. Deceased had had nothing to trouble her at the house,and there would have been no trouble at all If it was not for the drink. He (her father) would sometimes ask her mother when she was going to be a different woman and leave off the drink ; and he was sometimes very angry with her when she was drunk. He had also put her out of the bar when she proved annoying to the customers. This was done when deceased was drunk and it was then necessary. He was otherwise very kind to her. Her mother some- times seemed to be very distressed when witness's father was angry with her,and had said very often that she de- stroy herself. She had threatened to do away with would herself so many times that no notice was taken of her. It was impossible for her mother not to take drink. She seemed as though she could not do without it.—ln reply to the Coroner witness said her father never con- sulted a doctor about her only when ill. She had more than once been sent to Southport to recover, and had gone often of her own free will. Witness was always very kind to her mother when she was drunk and had never struck her. She had sometimes persuaded her to be sober. Elizabeth Brannon, domestic servant at the Anchor Inn, supported the statements of the previous witnesses. There was never any disturbance in the house only when the deceased took drink. Deceased had everything she wanted when sober, and had no cause to get drunk. - Joseph Bayley, lock keeper, said that at half-past nine o'clock on Saturday morning he saw a body in No. 2 lock of the Bridgewater Canal. He gave information to the police.. P.S. Woolley stated that having received information at quarter to ten from the last witness on Saturday morning he went to to the lock referred to, got a rake, and took the body from the water. It was completely rolled up like a ball, and at first it was thought that the head was missing. The legs and left arm were gone. The corsets produced were found in the fan of a small steamer behind which the body was found. The steamer must have evidently brought the body from a higher part of the canal. The corsets had been identified by Mr. Anderton as those worn last by his wife. The injury to the body must have been caused by the fan of the steamer. There was no suspicion as to the cause of her death. Witness had seen the deceased drunk four or fve years ago. He had no suspicion that the injuries were caused before death. The Coroner then reviewed the evidence at length,and having characterized the case as a very melancholy one, advised the jury to return a verdict of " Found dead," there being no evidence to show how the unfortunate woman had got into the canal. The jury returned a verdict of "Found dead" accord- ingly, and subsequently passed a vote of condolence to Mr. Anderton. DISCOVERY OF THE 'MISSING LIMBS. On Tuesday morning about nine o'clock the missing legs of, the deceased were found in the neighbourhood ef Top Locks, near to where the body had been discovered. They were taken from the water by the police and sub- sequently placed with the body in the coffin which was lying at the mortuary. THE INTERMENT OF THE DECEASED. UNSEEMLY T.-it 0 CEE DINGS.

The remains of the deceased Mrs. Anderton were in- terred on Tuesday afternoon in a grave situated in the Church of :England portion of the Runcorn Cemetery. Thetoffin containing the body was first taken from the mortuary and placed in a hearse which was then driven to the Anchor Inn, Brunswick-street. A large number of persons had collected round the house, and out of respect to the departed lady many window'blinds were drawn, and sevEral business houses were closed for a time. The hearse and four mourning coaches comprised the cortege, and at three o'clock it moved towards the Cemetery followed by sec.-oral hundred persons, the majority of whom were women. The crowd grew more dense the nearer the procession approached the Ceme- tery, and by the time this place was reached quite two thousand people had assembled. Sergeant ,Woolley with the assistance of severalcconstables succeeded in main- taining good order during the course of the service, at which the Rev. J. Holden officiated. Subsequent to the interment the proceedings .outside the Cemetery were of a very unseemly nature. As the coaches were leaving the burial place the crowd began to hoot and make use of language by no means to be approved of. This was maintained by certain individuals until the carriages were out of sight, as the speed at which the horses were driven made it impossible for those persons to keep up with them. A large number of the people ran in the direction of Brunswick-street, but before they arrived there some of the mourners had got into the house,whilst others had driven elsewhere. A renewal of the dis- graceful c,onduct outside of the house was evidently ex- pected, but beyond two or three feeble shouts, there was nothing of a disorderly nature, and shortly after- wards the neighbourhood was again quiet.
