'LIBERALISM AT WIDNES.

SPEECHES BY MESSRS. H. WADE DEACON AND J. FORETER.

On Wednesday night the annual meeting of the members or.: Widnes Liberal Club was held, under the presidency of Mr. Stuart -Deacon, when the officers were elected for theensuing year,and a very gratifying, report submitted of the work done during the past year. Subsequently a public meeting was held in in the club hall, when an address on ".The Democratic Work of the Government," was given by Mr. Councillor .E. Forster, JR., Liberal candidate for St. Helens. Mr. Stuart Deaconlpresided, and Mr. H. Wade Deacon, J.P., CJC.9 was also present.—The Chairman referred to the -probability of the general election taking place nemt year, and expressed the hope that the members of that club would be readyto do their duty when the time .came. They had only 1. majority of 030 against them and if they only put their shoulders to the wheel he lz:elieved they would return the Liberal candidate at the head of the pool. (Applause.) He trusted that they would see Mr. Forster in Paiiiament as the member for St. Helens, and there was also a gentleman present whom they hoped to see as member for the Widnes Division. (Fro - longed applause.)—W. Forster,who had a hearty recep- tion, said that the science of politics was to make every- body as comfortable as possible. The House of Com- mons and the House of Lords existed for that purpose,

and the members of those houses had no right to take into consideration their own rights and privileges. The present Government had done all they could to redeem the pledges they gave at the last election, and its action had been marked by a thoroughly democratic spirit. He then reviewed in detail the work of the Government,and expressed the hope that at the next general election they would have a substantial majority to carry on their work. (Applause.)—Mr. H. Wade Deacon proposed a vote of thanks to Mr. Forster, and spoke strongly in favour of a graduated system of taxation. The aim of political progress in the past had been political equality, which had now been secured, and he thought that in the future their aim would be to secure equality of social oppoctunity. (Hear, hear.) He forecasted various measures of reform, and said that it was very probable that in the future there would be a severance from the Liberal party of a good many people who were not in sympathy with them in many of their aspirations, but that severance would leave them stronger than ever to carry on their work.—The motion was seconded by Mr. Edward Carey and carried. MEASLES EPIDEMIC AT ST. HELENS.

===== 0002601_18941013_art0032.txt | included=true | included =====
CLOSING OF SCHOOLS.

A meeting of the St. Helens Health Committee was held on Wednesday afternoon, when Councillor J. Forster presided.—The medical officer (Dr. J. Robertson) reported that during the past fortnight there had been 106 births and 62 deaths, the latter being at the rate of 201 per 1,000 per annum,against 171 in the previous fortnight and 1415 in the corresponding period last year. The deaths from zymotic diseases were nine in number, and the notifica- tions of cases of sickness from zymotic diseases were 25 in number. Reports were presented showing that there had been seven cases of typhoid fever this year in the Eldon-street district, and a similar number of cases in Watery-lane, and the medical officer recommended im- provements in the sanitary condition of the houses. The medical officer also presented a report on the epidemic 'of measles in the town. He remarked that a similar epidemic in the spring of last year caused the death of 146 children, and the district of Parr suffered the most. The disease was again epidemic in that district, and its spread seemed to be largely due to one of the schools there. Of 'B4 children found sufferinc, from measles, no fewer than 75 attended, or had brothers or sisters attending, Holy Trinity Schools. On inquiry at the schools that morning, he found out of a total of 403 scholars 351 were in attendance, so that many were attending school front homes where other children were suffering from measles. An epidemic of measles was al- ways more fatal during the colder months, and it was, therefore, specially necessary that the present epidemic should be dealt with without delay.—Alderman J. C. Gamble asked if more good was done by allowing the children to run about the streets.—Dr. Robertson replied that medical men could not detect measles until a child had suffered for a day or two, and during that period children, if allowed to attend school, might be carrying the infection to others. He added that if the epidemic was dealt with energetically at once it might go away as rapidly as it had come.—The committee ultimately adopted a resolution requesting the closing ef Holy Trinity Schools for six weeks.

===== 0002601_18941013_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
DESERTING MILITIAMEN FREE IN THE ISLE OF MAN.

IMPORTANT JUDGMENT.

At the Staff of Government Court of the Isle of Man on Wedaesday, Governor Ridgeway presiding,judgment in an appeal case of considerable importance was given by Deemster Drinkwater. The original de- fendant,a militiaman named Looney, was charged with failing to appear at the annual training at Carlisle. He came to the Isle of Man, but the Manx justices refused to grant a summons on the ground that the Militia Act of 1882 did not apply to the island. An application was made to the Clerk of the Rolls for a mandamus to the justices to grant a summons, and this failed also. An apceal was then made to the Staff of Government Court. The decision of that court set forth that the Army Act of 1881 was made applicable to the Isle of Man, but the Militia Act of 1882 did not apply to the Isle of Man. Neither were certain provisions incor- porated in the Militia Act from the Army Act, and which was supposed to apply to this particular case, applicable to it at all. The appeal was therefore rightly dismissed. Thus deserting militiamen are free in the island.

BOOKS WE READ.

One of the most valuable little publications bristl- ing with interesting and instructive mattPr, including a carefully edited Calender for 1895, has been recently published by Messrs. Warner and Co., Limited, 88, Clerkenwell-road, London E.C. The first edition was a very large one, and no doubt its circulation will be several hundred thousand. The title" Bearers of Glad Tidings" is ex- ceedingly apropos, for it treats of an interesting subject, Some books are written to please Nur passing fancy for humour ; others for the student of the world's events others as instructors in political economy ; but this book treats of health, and tells how to obtain the maximum degree, of health, at a minimum cost. Professor J. Poiraud, 8.A., B.Sc., of Wargrave, Henley-on-Thames, writes :—" After reading the book published by Messrs. Warner and Co. (which is obtainable by a request on a postal card), I immediately procured a bottle of Warner's Safe Cure, and although weeks of treatment by the best London physicians failed to relieve me of inflammation of the bladder and prostrate gland, Warner's Safe Cure cured me, and now I can conscientiously say, my pre- sent good health is solely attributable to it." The care- ful painstaking reader always benefits by reading good books.

OUR LOCA.L CHURCHES-WARRINGTON.

THE SERVICES FOR THE PEOPLE.

In the Wesleyan Chapel,Bold-st.,on Sunday afternoon. the weekly service for the people was held. The Rev.G. D. Mason presided, and an interesting address was &li- vered by Mr. S. B. Stringer, who drew some lessons from the character and conduct of the prophet Jonah and God's dealings with him. He said they learnt from the experience of Jonah that it was a vain thing to attempt to fly from God. Their duty was sometimes painful to perform, but the easiest way was to face it manfully. God did not give His wayward servant up to himself, but he used severe measures in bringing him to a sense of his duty, which proved effectual. The or- chestral band played Mendelssohn's " War March of the Priests," and "The Better Land" was sung by Miss Kent and Mr. Cole gave a cornet solo, "The lost chord." The service throughout was bright and hearty,and there was a good attendance.

WYCLIFFE P.S.A. SOCIETY.

A meeting of this society was held on Sunday after- noon in Wycliffe Church and was largely attended. Mr. H. M. Ashton occupied the chair and briefly addressed the meeting. He related an incident showing the greater importance (A:deeds than words. Dr. Waite of Liverpool delivered the address for the afternoon. He denied the assertions of a recent writer that all the evil in the world was the product of ignorance. In support of his statement he referred to some of the present day evils arising from the drink traffic, and also to the long hours worked by shop assistants. He contended that it was not ignorance but the love of gain which was the cause of these evils. There was only one law which could bring men into right relationship one with another, whether in the case of a colliery owne r employing thousands of workpeople or the householder with her single domestic ' servant, and that law was "Thou shalt love that neigh- bour as Thyself." Dr. Waite's son afterwards well sang a solo entitled "Flee as a bird." The Rev. J. Yonge, Messrs. J. Irish and C. T. Owen also took part in the meeting.

===== 0002601_18941013_art0038.txt | included=true | included =====
SERMON ON MR. GLADSTONE'S ARTICLE ON HERESY AND SCHISM.

The Rev. W. H. Drummond, 8.A., in Cairo-st. Chapel on Sunday evening, took as the subject of his sermon Mr. Gladstone's article in the August number of the "Nineteenth Century," on "Heresy and Schism in the Modern Christian Church," basing his remarks on 1. Cor. xii. 4, 5. "Now there are diversities of gifts, but the same spirit, and there are differences of administration, but the same Lord." He said it was a pathetic sight to see Mr. Gladstone, having withdrawn from the heated strife of the political arena,spending a part of his closing days in endeavouring to draw men's attention to the vital doctrines of what he considered to be the true Church, and additional weight attached to his words , when it was remembered that ever since his Oxford days he had been considered as extremely learned in theology. Mr. Drummond then gave a brief epitome of Mr. Gladstune's article, quoting several of his more important propositions, and also his statements about English Nonconformity and the work it had done in , regard to the extinction of slavery. He also referred to the fact that Mr. Gladstme found comfort that after all the varying sections of the Church concurred in the essen- tials of the faith,and his plea fora readjustment of ideas, to encourage more mutual understanding by a survey of the whole facts of the case. Mr. Drummond said it was an article as amazing in its concessions as Its assump- tions. In charitableness it was in marked contrast to the spirit which was prevalent twenty or thirty years ago, which was something to be thankful fot. If the writer's position be true that the Church is a special organization with a priesthood and sacramental powers, then those who stood outside its pale suffered an eternal loss. But if this were so,they wanted to know where the true Church was to be found, and there was no attempt in the article to give enlightenment on that point, or to set up an infallible standard of doctrine, and without this all talk about heresy and schism was a mere beating of the air. But the whole question of heresy lay outside the Christianity of Christ. Speaking of the assumption that Christ founded a definite ecc!esiasticnl organization, and gave supernatural powers to its ministers, Mr. Drummond said that a careful reading of the gospels would afford no ground for it. In only two instances was the word " Church" used, and in one of those the above construction could certainly not be placed upon it. The test of Christianity lay not in doctrine but in char- acter,in the good life,and it was not denied that these ex- isted outside the pale of the Church. Christianity would never advance by asking who are the orthodox and who are the heretics. It was a barren controversy of the days of darkness. He urged his hearers to be Christians of to-day, and live for the service of the present hour. Two things Christianity needed : liberty and courage in thinking, and an increasing stress on character. It was only on the basis of character, love, the imitation of Christ, that the age-long dream of Christianity would be fulfilled, and there should be one fold and one shepherd.

AT CHESTER.

THE TROUBLES OF A. FRODSHAM MAX

"A RUDE AWAKENING."

Annie Lane, 11, Parry's-entry; William McDermott and Peter O'Brien (of no fixed residence) were charged at the Chester Police Court, on Friday, before Mr. Henry Churton and Mr. Geo.Dutton, the woman with stealing about £lB, tire money of Henry Hincle, Frodsham, and the two men with re- ceiving part of the money knowing it to have been stolen.

The Chief Constable stated that on Wednesday night the prosecutor met the female prisoner in City-road,and she took him tN a house in Parry's-entry, tenanted by Catherine Nevin, who was better known as "Kate the c:)ok." (Laughter.) In the morning the man found that his money had disappeared from his pocket. Some of the money had been recovered,and the two men were arrested at Crewe.

Henry Hinde, the prosecutor, stated that he lived at Frodbham, but he had been to Mold on Wednesday, and broke his return journey at Chester. He met the female prisoner about eleven o'clock, and she took him to Parry's-entry. He was not quite sober. In the morning he found that his money, amounting to between LA and £2O, had gone, and that the woman had gone also. (Laughter.) The money was composed of a£s note, slvereigns, half-sovereigns, silver, and copper. The £5 note he gave to a person in the house to change for him, but he did not see it again, (Laughter.) Irs answer to the female prisoner, prosecutor denied that he gave her £2 103., but stated that he gave her 10s. Detective Crewe deposed to arresting the two male prisoners at Crewe. They absconded from Chester when they knew that inquiries were being made about them. He found that they had boaght a new suit of clothes each, and that they had made several other extensive purchases, including second class railway tickets to Manchester. On McDermott he found £2 2s. 2d., and on O'Brien he found 4s. 40. McDermott said he found the £5 note produced in the entry. On this evidence the Chief Constable asked for a remand for a week,which was granted.

INTERESTING COUNTY COURT ACTION"

AT RUNCORN.

WHOSE WAS THE PROPERTY? At the Runcorn County Court on Tuesday after- noon before his honour Judge Wynne foulkes, an interpleader summons, which had created consider- able outside interest, was heard. The parties engaged were the plaintiff, Alfred Ellison, landlord of the "Man at the Wheel," Church-street, Runcorn; the defendant, Joseph Saunders, formerly a shopkeeper in. Cooper-street, Runcorn; and the claimant, Marguretta Mary Saunders, wife of the last natned person. The claim was for goods alleged to be the separate and abso- lute property of Mrs. Saunders, the greater part of which she had purchased out of her own private money and the others given to her. The claim was for £25, damages from the plaintiff the execution creditor) and the High Bailiff, for the seizure of the goods, the greater part of which was, it was alleged, not sold at fair prices, but nearly- given away. Mr. P. Davies, of Warrington. represented Mr. Ellison, Mr. H. R. S. Briggs, Manches- ter, appeared for claimant, and Mr. E. S. Giles, Chester, defended the High Bailiff. Mr. Briggs said that some time age Alfred Ellison ob- tained judgment against Joseph Saunders for £3 13s. The goods claimed for were 'seized on the 25th August, and on that day notice was duly served on the High Bailiff on behalf of the plaintiff. Nothing was heard of an interpleader summons having been issued but in re- ply to a wire he was informed that one had been issued and would be duly served. The articles seized for the execution levy of £4 is. 61 were valued at £33 is. ac- cording to purchase price and they were sold on the 3rd September.

His Honour saidthe appraisement was £8 10s. They could not claim damages from the High Bailiff for the difference in the value of the goods taken and the amount obtained. The ground of claim was that tne goods were considerably damaged by removal and that they were sold for inferior prices.

Mr. Briggs said that the goods were seized and what he complained of was that they ought to have had notice earlier than they did. They should have been notified forthwith on the interpleader summons having beea taken out. He further stated that the furniture had been purchased by Mrs. Saunders with money earned by her.

Marguretta Mary Saunders, the claimant, stated that she was living with her mother-in-law. She was married in October, 1887. The list of articles read over by her solicitor she testified as belonging to her. Some of them she had purchased before marriage, others had been given to her, and the remaining ones she had purchased after she was married with her own private money. Previous to her marriage she was in a situation as waitress at the Central restaurant, Oldham-street, Man- chester, for five years. After her marriage she took another situation as waitress at the Sale Botanical Gardens,where she remained for six months. She after- wards returned to Runcorn four years ago, and worked Ifor her mother-in-law at the" Fleetwood Arms " as bar- I maid. She remaintd there until twelve months ago when Ishe took a glop at 24, Cooper-street, and left it three months ago. Whilst in Manchester she was ill and being in want of money she disposed of her goods subsequently to Mr. John Evans, of Chapel-street, Salford, for £2O Ds. He afterwards let her have the goods back again under a hire agreement she paying him Bs. Bi. per week on the condition that when the full amount was paid the goods should again be her own. She had now discharged that liability and the goods were hers when they were seized by the bailiffs. She sold the goods to Mr. Evans about three years after her marriage and whilst she was living in Manchester. In reply to the Judge she said she could not estimate the amount of damage the furniture sustained.

Examined by Mr. Davies: At the time of her marriage her husband was in business in Market-st.' Manchester as a cigar merchant. She knew nothing of her busband's business and could not say how much capital he started business with. She was not aware of the nature of her husband's difficulties when he was in Manchester; neither did she know that his liabilities were nearly £l,OOO and his assets nil. Everything that had been seized belonged to her and had been paid for. She was present in the house when Mr. Torrie called on the 22nd August to take possession, but owing LI her being so much excited and astonished, she did not tell him that the goods were hers and not her husband's. Her mother-in-law was with her, but did not claim the property as her own. She only claimed a cuckoo clock and a portrait of herself. These, the men allowed to remain in the house. All that her mother said at the time to Mr. Torrie was that he would have to bring the things back again. Witness had not heard of her hus- band haying told a bailiiT that he had made over the furniture to her under a deed of gift. She had never. heard of a deed of gift by her husband. When she was married to him ho was in lodgings and had no furniture. Everything in the shape of household furniture belonged` absolutely to her. She knew nothing of her husband being forced to run away from Manchester owing to financial difficulties.

13v Mr. Giles: Some portion of the furniture was dam- aged when being taken by the County Court men down- stairs. She could not say what the names of the men were who were carrying them, nor how the claim was made up. It was prepared for her.

.e.. John Evans, of Chapel-street, Salford, sign writer, said that Mrs. Saunders who asked him in 1890 to enter into the agreement produced. He did so, purchasing the articles mentioned in that document. He executed the aereement, and allowed her to use the goods on hire. He agreed to her paying him Bs. 6d. per week and under- stood that when he was repaid the full amount,the goods became hers again. He had received the money. By Mr. Davies: Be knew Mr. Saunders before the lattn's marriage and was afterwards in the habit of visiting his house. fle knew Mr. Saunders met his creditors in 1889. He conapleted the transaction in good faith and thought it was for a good cause. They took the furniture with them from Manchester to Runcorn. By His Honour: He did not know what means Mrs. Saunders had of paying him back, but trusted to her honesty. There was nothing between thorn to show where she was to get her money from. By Mr. Davies: Both Mr. and Mrs. Saunders were present when the agreement was entered into. Mrs. Saunders, however, did all the talking. (Langhter.) Mrs. Martha Oakes, of 4, Clarence-street, Runcorn, deposed to gluing Mrs. Saunders £lO on her marriage. Her daughter-in-law had stored her goods at the "Fleet- wood Arms" a portion of the time while acting as bar- maid. She gave her 10s. per week because she was so honest. She remained with lier until the Ist of October last year. Witness had since made her several presents. Mr. Davies desired to draw attention to the way in which the matter had been put before the court. He thought the evidence of the claimant was very contradic- tery from beginning to end, and that it could not be relied upon. Three claims had been made for the goods seized, and it was stated by Mrs. Oakes, at the time of the seizure, and in the presence of the claimant, that the articles were hers. He asked his honour to discredit the statement that tbe goods belonged to the claimant. They belonged, undoulitedly to Mr. Saunders. When the husband came home the Saturday fellowing the seizure he saw Mr. Lyons and told him that he had (riven the goods to his wife by a deed of gift prepared by Mr. Briggs. Alfred Torrie, sub-bailiff of the Runcorn County Court, proved taking possession of the house occupied by the claimant. Exceptional care was taken in the re- moval of the goods. Mr. John Cyons deposed to his beink told by Mr. • Saunders that he had given the goods to his wife by a deed of gift before leaving Manchester. His Honour said the high bailiff was net responsible for the process obtained. The claimant ought, in order tap prove her case,produce evidence to show that she had purchased the goods before marriage, or by her own money. No such conclusive evidence, however, had been given. Under the circumstances he gave judgment for the execution creditor, with costs on the higher scale. Mr. Briggs asked ler a case, and gave notice of appeal.

Till& Isle of Man will now, no doubt, , The DeUntie- be regazdA as the refuge of Militia- man's Refuge. men who have tired of the annual drilling. It has been decided by the highest court in the island that a deserting militiaman can not be arrested so long as he keeps his feet firmly planted on Manx soil. This state of affairs, which must be rather embarrassing so far as the military authorities are concerned,is caused by an oversight or a blunder in drafting an Act of Parliament. It appears that the Army Act of 1881 was made ap- plicable to the Isle of Man, but the framers of the. 'Militia Act of 1882 seen to have forgotten that the ;little island in the Irish Sea forms part of the IBritish Empire. They have now been rather rudely reminded of the fact that there is an Isle of Man, and that it has its own courts and Deem- sters to give judgments according to its own laws.. :The militiaman, Looney, who invoked the inter- ferenc.e of the Manx courts will hereafter—amongst militiamen at all events—be one of the world's famous refugees. At the present time it seems to be as difficult to obtain" extradition " in his case •as in that of the famous Jabez.

The "Nonconformist Con- The "Nonconformist Conscience." science " is sometimes sneered at by those who have no clear apprehension of the real meaning of the Phrase, but at the same time it has its defenders even in unexpected quarters. For instance, at the Church Congress of all places, the Rev. J.E. C. WELLDON, the Head Master of Harrow, spoke not only respectfully but oven approvingly of it. As Mr. WELLDON points out, the expression, "Noncon- formist Conscience," is really the name given to a remarkable rising of public opinion" which has , helped to purify and sweeten the political and commercial life of the country. There are people who say that the "Nonconformist Conscience" is foolish or vindictive, but Mr. WELLDON is not of that opinion. "So far as I can see,it has," be says, " until lac-by lcn rimjiy thc: utterancc., cf a disin-

1 terested an enlightened moral sense; it has been t. an outburst of 3. just and righteous indignation." It has now mgd :r "that the moral sense of the e el!,ok nation will not the affront of notorious stfiti4 sensaality in a promiinesa public man, be he , statesman or administrator; OT ,nrince." In the Opinion of this prominent Churel'irer.o it is not the Nonconformists who need to be- ashaqn oc': of having a public conscience, but the Church of Eng-1211d for not having had more of it.

THE war in the Enat de:reloPes The War in the East. elowly, There is, it if tru,ls no

lack of news of the progress Pf even published in the daily newspapers,. but' the newt is ;unreliable. 7here isa little information to be deriwxf from the actual seat of war,except what cornegfrom one or other of the combatants-,.and neither the Chinese no Sapertese seem to'have the strictest regard for accuracy. Two or throe. weeks- ago- there were full details reported of' a: great Xapanese victory, bnt,, according to the. Chinese, these details were, for the most part, the' products of a vivid imagination. The Celestials do' not enctly deny that an encounter of some kind or another did take place, but they allege that it was only a small affair, and that none. of their best troops were engaged. It seems certain,. however, that when the troops of the two countries; do meet the suberior discipline and leadership of the. Japan- ese give there the victory. They are being aided too, by Chinese dishonesty, for it has- been found that the great stores of rifles, and ammunition that it was supposed' had been bought are really non-existent. The money' that should have been expended in preparationefor war has gone into the pockets of thievish officints. Now, however, the Chinese seem to be making-- desperate efforts to repair the effects of past mis- management, and, considering the immense - resources and population of their country, it is quite possible that they may yet turn the tables' upon the Japanese, if the latter do not strik& quick:y and strike hard..

By Messrs. T. J. L. TUNSTALL 4- CO.

Christian SOMIS interesting particulars of the Endeavour origin and growth of the Christian Societies. Endeavour movement was given in it paper read at the meeting of the Baptist Union, in, Newcastle last week. The effects of the movement were certainly not minimised by the writer of the paper, the Rev. J. STUART, of Wat- ford, for he‘ declared that "for good or ill, it had opened, a, new page in Church history." The first Christian Endeavour Society was founded in Portland Maine, and the problem which its originator, Dr. CLARKE, stt hi mself to solve was "how to give everyone some proper work fitted to his power and years." The growth of the move- ment has, it appears, been phenomenal. In thirteen years there were 28,696 branches in the United States, 2,243 in puma da, and 1,453 in Great Britain. The Baptists are in the van in this country with 391 branches, the Congregation, alists coming next with 353, and the Methodists with 231. The speakers who debated the paper seem to have been unanimous in commendation of the work done by the Socieities.

DROWNING FATALITY AT WARRINGTON. 7- 1-

• STRANGE IDEAS OF A WITNESS. The inquest upon the body of Ann Chamber:din,. of Orford, whose body we found in the river Mersey near Arpley meadows, on Friday afternoon,. was held on Tuesday at the Black Horse Inn,Sankey Bridges, by Mr. S. Brighouse.. .James Chamberlain, the son of the deceased, said thz.ts he was a postman and resided with his mother at Orford. Deceased was 71. years of age, and she was widow. He last saw her alive on September 26th, at night, when she expressed her intention of going to see her daughter, Mrs. 'faker, in Lovely-lane. He never, saw her, alive again. She never got to Mrs. Taker's - house. He saw her body on Saturday night. James Watkinson, 44; Leonard-st., said that he saw the deceased at eight o'clock on the night of September 26th, near Orford Hall. She said that she was going to see her daughter. He went with her as far as Battrsby- lane, and then left her. The night was very dark. Alfred Gough, 7, Balfour-at., Runcorn, captain of the tug boat Heywood, belonging to the Manchester Ship Canal, said that at about 2.30, on the previous Friday afternoon he saw the body of the deceased floating dowry the river Mersey, near Walton Arches. He took the body up the river, and fastened it to the bank and then sent for the police. The Coroner : What did you do that for ? Witness : I was not aware that we could take the body out of the water until the. police were present. (Laughter.)

, , The Corner: What ideas some people have ! But suppose there was probably some life, would you then wait until the police came. Witness: I should have taken it out then. Sergeant Devlin: A note was sent to the police at Warrington, and they sent to us. We got there at five o'clock. The Coroner : Why the body. would be in the river almost two hours! How was it fastened to the bank? Sergeant Devlin: There was a rope round the body, and it was made secure by fastening it to a stake in the bank. The Coroner: This is a strange idea. P.C. Dugdale said that he found the body some dis- tance above Walton Arches. When searched, ss. 20. was found in the pockets of the deceased, and a small bottle of whiskey. There were no marks of violence. Sergeant Devlin said that the body had been towed up the river for a quarter of a mile before it was secured to the bank.

The Coroner: You see sargeant,, how these people are afraid of you. You ought to be quite pleased. (Laughter.) The jury returned an open verdict of "Found drowned."'

"THE DEMOSTHENES OP METHODISM."

LECTURE AT WARRINGTON. The committee of the Young People's Society of Christian Endeavour in connection with the Bold- street Wesleyan Chapel, held the ETst of a series of lectures, which they intend to carry out during the winter months, on Monday evening. The lecturer on that occasion was the Rev. Dinsdale T. Young, of Manchester, one of the rising ministers in Wesleyan Methodism, and he chose as his subject," The Demosthenes of Methoiisrnr being a sketch of the life of the Rev. Samuel Bradburnone of the early ministers in the Wesleyaß Methodist connection. There was a good attendance. in the schoolroom to hear the rev.

kentleman, and the chair was occupied by the superin- tendent of the Sunday School who briefly introduced the lecturer.

The Rev.. D. T. Youndin.introducing his subject said that the history of Methodism was a marvellous history. It was a . istory of a splendid seirizual triumph, and it presented strange blending of noble, intellectual, and fervent spiritual life in which,the heroic had always keen conspicuous. There were-some pessimists who said that there was nothing of the heroic in modern Metho- dism,.but he believed that was slanderous, and that there were in many a rural district and in many a. seething city many exemples, of Methodist heroism. He thought that they ought to know more of the early Methodist preachers of 1.50 years ago. From despised begianings,they became under divine providence,a Chris- tian church which had spread in almost every part of the world. Connected with this evangelistic battle was the name of Samuel Bradburn. This man was conspicuous for his usefulness, and by his labours he turned many to righteousness. He laboured constantly in the Metho- dist ministry for upwards of 40 years, and he was always esteemed the Demosthenes of early Methodism. In these days when the doctrines of heredity were so much preached,. it was always necessary to give an. account of a man's paretiege. 'Quite a romance could be written of the life of Bradburn's father and mother. In the year 1740, in the old city of Chester, they were• married,,without the consent of their parents, and with- out any prospect of, being able to support a family. Black, clouds quickly gathered,and one night while in a public house, he found that a shilling had been slipped into his pocket, and that he was thereby enlisted in the army. Although he endeavoured to prove that he had not accepted the shilling, he was compelled to go, and-his wife went with him. "Sammy," as the subject of.the lecture was always known, was born just as the vessel bearing the regiment in which his father was drafted, hove in sight of Gibraltar. Twelve years were spent in Gibraltar, and then Bradburn's parents removed, to Chester where at the age of thirteen years he. was apprenticed to a cobbler. His history of four or five years was a very dark one for he plunged into all kinds. of vice, but he fortunately changed his master and worked for a Christian cobbler, by whom he became so impressed with the truths of Christianity that he be- came a convert and joined the Methodist Church. On February 7th, 1773, he happened to be at a Methodist chapel in Wrexham, and as the local preachen appointed) did not arrive, the chapel steward asked " Sammy" te occupy the vacant pulpit. After a little hesitation he did so, and, notwithstanding the absence of. grammars his sermon was so powerful that at night the chapel vial; crowded to hear him. This was the commencement of his ministerial labours, and he was finall7 taken into the ministry, where he became a wonderful power for good- In 1799 lie was elected President of the Conference, the highest position in Methodism. The iecturer described these various details with great eloquence, and then gave a number of humorous anecdotes which were told of Samuel Bradhurn. With great pathos he told of Bradburn's fall throtieh drink alter he had passed the president's chair. This fall was however only ternP9r- ary, and he died honoured by all in July 26, 1816. Bs was buried next to his great and beloved friend, John Wesley, and was followed to his grave by the memberS of the conference which was sitting at the time in City- road Chapel, London. At the close of the lecture a hearty vote of thanks was accorded the Rev. D. T. Young for his eloquent address. and the hope was expressed that he would ceme again before long. The meeting was breu:ht to a cicEe by the 0. Liw Leueutctivu.

THINGS HEARD AND SEEN AT RUNCORN.

BY AN OCCASIONAL CONTRIBUTOR.

It is generally known that we have typhoid fever in our midst, but it is not so much a matter of general information that our Health Committee refuses to admit persons suffering from this disease into the hospital on Runcorn Hill. Yet it. is a fact that the leading lights of our Health Committee do not consider it necessary for the health of the town to isolate these cases, and so our medical men are left to do what they can by themselves•to stamp out the disease. At the beginning of the present week a woman died from the complaint, and the circumstances of this case, which are of a very melancholy nature, might be urged in favour of the hospital being thrown open for the , purpose cited above. It must not be thought that this was the only case which ought, out of absolute necessity, to have been removed to the isolation hospital for there are unhappily many more of an equally dangerous nature. Some two months ago the woman alluded to had passed through a very , severe illness and soon after forsaking her bed— which she did several weeks too soon in order to attend to her childrens' wants—one of the children I and her husband became ill with typhoid fever and had to take to their bed. Unfortunately, this bed had apparently to serve the uses of all the members of the family, numbering six, and was situated in the kitchen, where all meals we re prepared and eaten. So that while the two were struggling through the disease the other children and the mother, who was of course still very weak, were sleeping in the bed, and breathing the same air. The husband managed to rally through and got up, but no sooner had he done so than his wife was seized with the illness. Two of the other children were next affected, and the hus- band,who had only reached the convalescent stage, had to administer to the wants of all, and get what little rest he could. There were,then,as will readily be seen, four persons suffering from the fever at once; all sleeping in one bed, and that placed in ' none too large a room.

It was impossible for these poor people to get proper rest at any time with their bodies all cramped into one bed, and the mother often ex- pressed the wish that the hospital would receive some of her children in order to give them and her- self a fair chance to recover. But although the children gradually got better the mother got worse until on Monday last she expired. Is this son of thing* continue whilst the ratepayers have the means close at hand of saving these poor unfortun- ate beings? Surely these are cases which need iso- lation and the ratepayers would not be very great losers by such cases being taken to the hospital. It is the height of folly to keep a hospital closed against such cases because of a bit of a squabble between the Guardians and Commissioners as to who shall pay. The town should be kept healtlay.therefore the committee should do their best to rid us of the disease notwithstanding its attendant expense. If this is not done, and very soon too, we shall find ourselves mixed up with such a collection of diseases that the very doctors will despair of curing us.

The Work of a district nurse is by no means light, and judging from the remarks of some of the speakers at the recent meeting of the subscribers to the Runcorn Nurse Fund, the lay who holds that position in this town has her hands full. She is evidently an enthusiast and manifests the deepest sympathy with her more unfortunate fellow crea- tures. There seems to be no higher sphere of labour for woman than that of "healing the sick," and surely that ought to be high enough to satisfy the most ambitious of our ladies. Every woman is a nurse, only perhaps not in the same degree as the one of whom we have spoken; i and it would truly go hard with us f woman, in some of her present day airy flights, were to fly be- yond the sphere of nursing. Doctors are every day expressing their warm approbation of every separ- ate district having its nurse, and some of these medical men frankly admit that their own skill would not count for much in some cases if a nurse was not in attendance. Therefore the object of raising sufficient funds for the maintenance of the nurse in Runcorn is a worthy one; and the appeals for assistance will, we hope, not go unanswered.

To hear some of the tales that are going the rounds of Runcorn would raise a doubt in certain people's minds that they are living in the nineteenth century : the age of civilization and progress. The circumstances connected with the death of the late Mrs. Anderton are sad in the extreme, and have formed the chief topic of 'conversation and specu- lation amongst the inhabitants of the neighbour- hood wherein she had resided. At the coroner's inquiry several witnesses testified to having known the deceased woman quite intimately and gave evidence of their identification of the woman's features. Other undoubted evidences were brought forward, and on Tuesday afternoon the remains were interred in the cemetery. Sub- sequent to this ceremony there was a most dis- graceful scene inside and outside of the cemetery gates. \Some two to three thousand persons had ' gathered together, and as the carriages left the burial ground, they hooted loudly and made use of very ill-selected words. On Wednesday night there was a rumour that the deceased woman had been seen that night in the bar of the " Anchor Inn," drawing beer ; whilst others said that her body had again been found at Preston Brook. One woman emphatically stated that she had seen Mrs. Anderton in the flesh, giving the silver spoons, which she had taken from the house on the night of her disappearance, away to some of her acquaintances, it is really astonishing the amount of credence which was given to these tales, and as a consequence of their spreading several hundreds of people visited the "Anchor Inn" that same night with the view of catching a glimpse as they said, of the deceased 'lady. When are we going to cease believing in the supernatural ?

ACCIDENT TO A FOOTBALL FLA:MR AT NEWTON. On Saturday afternoon during the progress or a match betwen Newton-le-Willows reserve and Penketh reserve, Enoch Millington, one of the Newton players, collided with one of his opponents, end fell to the ground. A LAGGARD LOVER AT RUNCORN.

A SUDDEN CHANGE OF MIND.

A young man hailing from Mossley, near Old- ham, having won the affections of a young Runcorn lady, "popped the question," and was accepted. Prepartions were at once made for providing a suitable dwelling, and when all was complete the banns were announced in the Runcorn Parish Church the requisite number of times. The wedding ceremony was announced to hake place at the church mentioned on Wednesday aft ernoon shortly after two o'clock, The prospec- tive bridegroom, accompanied by his mother jour- neyed to Runcorn from Motmtey,and upon arrival at 1 the bride's house, attired themselves in their best 1 apparel for he event. Preparations also went for- ward merrily with the other members of the wedding party, the bride of conrse being the centre of attention. The intending bridegroom betook himself in apparent glee to Messrs-. Tarwood's stables and ordered the necessary conveyances,. and at about one o'ci'ack everything was in perfect readiness. Suddenly the young an both ought himself thar he bad not had time to iaduilge in oe shave that morning owf,ng to the program- af more weighty matters, and immediately expressed' his intention of going to a !lairdresser's for this purpose.. He went.. At two o'albck promptly the wedding carriages, 'tour in number,,puiled up at the door, but no bride— groom had arrived. Everyone was ready bit' of coursa couki not think of lerving the.l hAuhe minus this very necessary young man..l 'The minutes flew by until a quarter to three o'clock and. no 'A.ece of the young man having been found it was decided to send the aarriages back. A messenger had been, previously sent in great haste to the church to see if by choince the required personage was patiently waiting there,.l but the only person found there, waiting patiently, was the I.Zev. Mr. Jameson, who was to have performed the marriage ceremony.. (Shortly before three o'clock, thiks gentleman also left. A search was immediately made for the missing lover andfrom inquiries it was found that he had obtained a shave at a local barber's not far from the house. Efferts to trace his aubse- quent movements, were however, fruitless,. The occurrence has- had a very bad effect upon ! the bride-elect, and she was attended by a medical man who found her in a prostrate state.. !The affair has created quite a sensation iti the neighbourhood of the bride's dweliling and is being ! generally spoken about. NOTES BY THE WAY AT EARLESTOWN.

BY AN OCCASIONAL CORM3:SPONDItNT.

I am sorry to Ind that Nurse Wilson,. the dis- trict nurse for the Medical and Surgical Aid Society, has resigned her position. During her stay in the neighbourhood she has, done an immense amount of good, and her visits have proved nothing short of a blessing in many house- hold . Still the work was not exactly what she expected it to be, and consequently she was some- what disappointed. In addition to this other little matters affecting the inner working of the Society, cropped up, and taking all things into considera- tion the nurse considered her only course, was to resign the position, much to the regret of the Com- mittee.

Another nurse has been appointed—Mrs. Jones, who has charge of the depot, and in my opinion the committee have made a very suitable choice. Mrs. Jones is a certificated nurse having served her probation at the Manchester Infirmary, and also acted in the capacity of private nurse on the staff, in outside cases. Of course, as I said, when first the question of appointing a nurse was brought up, the committee must take care not to interfere too much with her in the execution of her duties. To all intents and purposes she is public property, the committee simply acting as the representatives of the public, and it should be distinctly understood that she does not belong to any one medical man. It is absurd to suppose for one moment that an' medical man can appropriate her services for his patients alone. Each and all have an equal claim upon her, but her attendance should only be re- quired at cases where professional aid is needed. If the committee wish to steer clear of rocks, they must keep this ever before them.

Since the public meeting of ratepayers held a week or two back, respecting the ques- tion of how best to select candidates for the District Council, matters have been rather quiet, although I believe the various ward Committees have met and advanced matters a stage. Rumours there are, in plenty, but what foundatiln they have in fact, I cannot at present say. As I said before,the committees are so mixed, both as regards politics and creed, that it will sur- prise me me very much if they are able to come be- fore the ratepayers again, and say they have suc- ceeded in selecting their candidates. I hear already that one or two of the committees art, not exactly " happy.families," and things are not going quite so pleasantly as one would wish. If all be true, however, I cannot see of what use the Ward Com- mittees will be, for when they have said and done all they can, the thing will without a doubt be fought on strictly party lines. We want 15 coun- cillors, but already, with the Conservative selec- tions, the ward's committees selections, and so- called Independent candidates, we have something like double that number, ready and willing to sacrifice their peace of mind for the benefit of the " workingman."

I almost forgot to mention that the "new woman "craze has found its way into Earlestown, and we have now a few advocates making strenu- ous efforts to take women from their proper sphere, the domestic hearth, and put her on a level with that presumptuous creature, called " man." Of course they are feeling their way very carefully just at first, in order to see in what spirit their claims are received, but they do not intend to re- main long in the background, and as a preliminary they have seized the present opportunity of circu- lating broadcast some literature on "The Election of Women on Parish and District Councils." I have heard the names of two ladies who are to be brought forward in competition with the inferior article, as candidaMs for the Council, and if the result rested with the women, well,they might get elected.

NOTES FROM LIVERPOOL AND DISTRICT.

(BY A LIMB OF THF.. LAW.)

Birkenhead is in the throes of an electiom. MC W. H. Lever, one of the well-known manufacturers of "Sunlight" soap is the Liberal candidate, and he has a very good chance of succeeding. Birkenhead I believe has never yet returned a Liberal member, but the Liberal party ate very sanguine this time. The Levers are very popular as they have not only been generous to Birkenhead hut have shown in several ways practical sympathy with their em- ploy6s. Their business has recently been turned into a Limited Company. The works which are plemsantly situated in the country near to Brom- boro' Pool now employ upwards of 1,500 men and boys. They have also erected a considerable num- ber of houses in which their workmen and families reside.

There is a prevailing idea that Liverpool is one of the most drunken places in England. I often think this is a most erroneous idea. I have Wen in most parts of the city and my experience is that there is in proportion to population less drunkenness than in the manufacturing towns of Lancashire. It is a rare sight indeed to see a drunken man in the chief streets of the city in the business part of the day.

One of the American Line of Steamers intend to build two more steamers—of course they mean to "lick creation." Yankees always state that and these steamers are going to be superior to any others afloat. As it is intended to build them in America the probability is that they will not suc- ceed as it is proverbial that the "Yankees have not yet been successful in building steamers." Those two fine steamers the " Campania" and " Lucania " are running splendidly and making " record " passages. To see them on the Mersey one can hardly realise their stupendous size and how easily a man's hand can guide them. Their length is 620 feet (i.e. over 206 yards long), breadth 65 feet 3ins.. depth 43 feet, tonnage 12,950, horse power 30,000 accomodation 1,400 passengers besides of course luggage. The funnels of these steamers are of great size their inside diameter being rather more than 19 feet and to give an idea of their size it will be found that if they wer tunnels ine-09,1 ef tu-ne's that a railway train can pass dowa :hem.

UNWErrINSODIE FISK .8,,T Sr. HELENS.

William Coles,. of Tontine-St., St. Helens,a fish dealer,, was summoned, but did not appear, at the St. Halms Police Court, on Tuesday, for having exposed fisn for sale in College-4t., on the Ist inst., the said fish being putrid and unfit for the food of man. Mr. W. J. Jeeves crown Clerk) prosecuted. Meat Inspector Smirthwaite stated that he examined the haddies and mussels which the defendant was offering for sale, and he found, the mussels in a rotten and putrid condition. They were taken to the Town Hall, where Dr. Robertson con- demned them, and Mr. John Cotton, J.P., orderedl them to be destroyed. Dr. Robertson said of all forms of food putrid mussels were the mast dangerous and likely to produce serious disease. Chief Constable Wood having informed the magistrates %Alderman Harrison and Mr. W. J. Thomson) that the defendant was convicted in August last for a similar offence, defendant was com- mitted to prison for six weeks without the option of a fine.

===== 0002601_18941013_art0070.txt | included=true | included =====
COTTAGE PROPERY OWEIRRS' ASSOCLAVION.

The first annual meeting of the St. Helens and District Cottage Property Owners' Association was held on Wed- nesday night, at the Central Cafe, Bridge-street, St. Helens, Councillor Isaac B.Rose presiding, There was a good attendance of members. The annual report of the committee intimated that,in the opinion of its znombers, the association had carried out the original intention aimed at when formed. Legal advice had been taken upon such mattera as the reduction of the income tax on property, on the rights of auctioneers to remove goods, and also as to the payment of rates, taxes itc. Duputations had waited upon the Finance and Watch Committees witn successful results ; also upon the Water Committee in the hope of obtaining a discount on water rates, but although not successful at the time a promise was given that it should be considered next year. The St. Helens district had become affiliated with the society in London, and also with the Liverpool Association. The membership of the St. Helens District Association represents the owners of about 4,000 cottages in St. Helens. The report and balance-sheet was unanimously adoptcd, and the election of officers and committee took place.

SF. JOHN'S, WALTON.

The annual harvest thanksgiving services in connection with the above church were held on Sunday. The preacher, morning and evening, was the Rev. Francis Jourdain, vicar of Ashbourne, Derbyshire. The musical portion of the service was very well rendered under the direction of Mr. Lane, the organist. The anthem sung was " 0 taste and see." The collection was in aid of the Warrington Infirmary. In the evening there was an exceptionally large congregation, and the discourse of the preacher was much appreciated. An organ recital was given in the afternoon by Mr. Lane and proved very enjoyable. The solos were rendered in an excellent manna by Mr. W. S. Howard. The programme was as follows :—Prelude and Fugue in D minor (Mendelssohn), Impromptu (Archer), Air with variations in A (flessei ; vocal solo "Comfort ye," " Every valley" (Hande ), Mr, Howard; Meditation (Manly), Poatlude in D (H. Smart), Intermezn (MaScagni), March in E flat (Wely).

CEIRIST_CHURCH, CROFT.

The harvest festival in connection with Christ Church. Croft, were commenced on Friday evening. There was a fair congregation at the service which commenced with the signing of the well-known harvest hymn. "Praise, 0 praise our God and King." The service was intoned by the Rev. H. I. Wadesen, rector, and the Rev. J. J. Langham, vicar of Padgate, read the lessons and preached the sermon. The choir gave an excellent rendering of C. Simper's Magnificat service in F, and the anthem "0 Lord, how manifold are Thy works." The soprano solo was sung by Miss Maines. The services were continued on Sunday, when there were good congregations. The Rector again intoned the prayers at each service, and the lessems morning and afternoon were read by the Rev. R. Tebbs, vicar of St. 'Thomas's Church,Wigan, who also pl'eached the sermons. The Recto: preached in the eveniag. Special psalms and hymns were again sung. Mr. I. Cawthorne, choir- master, was at the organ. The offertories were in aid of the church expenses, the total amount realized at the four services being £ll Os. id. BOLD-STREET WESLEYAN CHAPEL.

The annual harvest festival in connection with the above cl:apel was held on Sunday, when there were excellent congregations both morning and evening. The preacher in the morning was the Rev. Owen Watkins whk, preached an appropriate sermon. During the ser- vice, the choir rendered the anthem, "Thou crownest the year," in an excellent manner. In the evening, the large chapel was crowded to its utmost limits, and the preacher was the Rev. Edward Parry, of Nantwich,who preached an able sermon upon the subject of "Life." The preacher pointed out that no painter could infuse life into his canvas. The sculptor commenced to work on a rough block of stone, and used the hammer and the chisel, and by the aid of imagination and genius fas- hioned the human face divine, but he could never infuse

life into his work. Man could do many things. His inventions were the wonder of the age, yet he could never give life even to the smallest insect. And the same want of power applied to the seed. The seed was placed in the ground, and winter's frost, snow, and rain descended. The grain was buried in the soil,and having vitality it burst its shell ant sprang fortl-4---" first the blade, then the ear, after that the full coin in the ear." The rev, gentleman observed that there was nothing ugly in the universe of God ; God never made anything that was ugh. There was only one thing ugly and that was sin. Nor was there anything so beautiful as holi- ness. Two anthems were again ably rendered by the choir, the first being "Sing unto the Lord with thanksgiving," and the second, " 0 Taste and see." Mr. Harrison presided at the organ with his usual efficiency. Collections were taken at each ser- vice. The communion rail and pulPit, and various parts of the chapel were beautifully decorated with flowers, ferns, fruits and vegetables, and thw. worlerefleeted great creAit decora'ers,,thp_ceminu-len rail and pul- p.. MUNICIPAL ELECTIONS.

THE TRADES COUNCIL AND THE ELECTIONS_ A special meeting of the Warrington Trades Counc.fil was held at the Cocoa, Rooms, Horsemarket-street, en- Tuesday evening, to consider the course to be pursued in the forthcoming municipal electioEs. At their meet- ing a fortnight previously it had been decided to •nomin— ate Mr. allaines Battersby in opposition, to Mr. Plumptors in the Whitecross wart and they also chose candidates for the Bewsev and St.. John's ward far the election to the Board of Guardians-. The meeting on Tuesday was called to decide whetherit was advisable to proceed with the contest in the Whitecross ward, or whether it would • be better to ,concentrate the energies of-the ;Trades Coun- cil upon. elections.. Mere was a good number of delegates- present, and/the chair was-occupied by Mr. T: Malkin,

Previoua.to the special business being_ proceeded with, Mr. Bullock, the secretary., said that he had received a letter thom. Mr. John Haley, on behalf of the Labour Committee of the Warrington Social Questions Union, with regard to the formation of a Conciliation Board for Warrington. It will be remembered that about 18 months ago steps were taken to foam a Conciliation Board,ltut the negotiations-for some reason or other fel through. The letter now stated that the difficulty for- merly experienced had been considered by the Labour Committee, and it was suggested tbat deputations from that committee, the Trades Council, and the Chamber of Commerce should meet to discuss the matter,and prob- ably the d:ffrculty would be overcome.—lt was resolved ta communicate with the Labour Committee to the effect that the Trades Council viewed the proposal with favour, and would adopt any arrangement that might be arrived at by the committee for securing the end in view. The following deputation was appointed to represent the Council, Messrs., Malkin (president), Bullock (secretary), S. Wright, J.P., Ogden and Battersby. Coming to the special business of the evening, Mr. Malkin said that they all knew the position of,the Coun- cil with reference to the election in the Whitecross ward, and he hoped that whatever was decided that evening, would be taken up in earnest. He regretted that at the last election in Howley some of their members were cot upon the scene as he would have liked, but work, no doubt, prevented them,. He thought that if they entered upon the contest in the Whitecross ward, they had very favourable prospects of gaining the seat. Mr. GARNER observed that the fact c,f the political parties having compromised the matter did not affect them at all.

Mr. 13ArrEnsny said that he did not think the two parties would combine together against independent candidates as they did in other towns. He thought that it was the town councillors who were interested who had made the compromise. At the some time should the Trades Council desire him to withdraw from the contest, he was perfectly willing to do so. Mr. Brnuocit said that under certain conditions it would not be disho,nour be to retire from.the contest. If the majority felt disposed to go on he would throw in his lot with them, hut still he thought it would be better to reserve their energies for the contest for the 13card of Guardians.

Mr. S. WRIGHT, J.P., said that he was of the same opinion, although he was stronfey opposed to Mr. Plumpton. lie was however of opinion they ought to husblnd their resources. After further discussion Mr. E. Edwardson moved, and Mr. Ogden seconded, that Mr. Battersby be candi. date for Whitecross Ward in opposition to Councillor Plumptnn. Mr. BARNES moved and Mr. Bullock seconded an amendment that Mr. Battersby's candidature be with- drawn, and that the ward be contested at the guardians election in December.

The amendment only received the votes of the mover and seconder, and the motion was therefore carried by a large majority. Mr. BATTERSEY expressed his hope that he would be successful at the election, and preparations were at 'once made to prosecute a vigorous canvas of the ward. It may be mentioned that Mr. Lang has been selected for the St. John's ward, and Mr. Malkin for the Bewsey ward for the forthcoming electios, for the Board of Guar- dians.

LYMM.

ANNIVERSARY SERMONS.. - The anniversary sermons took place on Sunday in the Lymm Wesleyan Chapel. The offertories were set apart for the trust funds.

NARROW ESCAPE FROM DROWNING.—About ten o'clock on Sunday night, Charles Ogden, a Lyrnm resident, was going home along the canal bank when he accidentally fell into the water. His cries speedily brought Mr. James Whitelegg on the scene, and he pluckily jumped into the canal to render assistance. The night was very dark and both men were in great danger. They were assisted to the bank by Sergeant Wynne and Mr. George Moston and were both very much exhausted. DWELLING-HOUSE FIRE.— Ei fire broke out on Sunday morning in the dwelling-house of Mr. James Blease, Legh-street. Blease and his wife had retired on Satur- day night, taking with them into the bedroom a lighted candle, and it is supposed that the candle was not properly extinguished, and that sparks from it caught in the bed clothing in the room. About two o'clock on Sunday morning, Mrs. Blease was awakened by being burnt on her arms and face. She saw the room was full of smoke, and screamed out "fire." She ran downstairs to procure help. Assistance was t hand, and a ladder was brought and run up to the bedroom window. There was no damage done to the property, but the bed cloth- ing and wearing ppparel ware destroyed. It is very fortunate that the fire was discovered, as the inmates might have been burnt to death.

A NON-SUPPING HORSESHOE.-Mr. E. de Dorsey, of 44, Castle-street, Liverpool, has invented a horseshoe to prevent slipping under any circumstances. The shoe consists of the ordinary iron hoof protector, to which a stout pad of rubber is fixed. In order to insert the pad so that it shall be secure to the last day on which the shoe remains on the horse, the iron is hollowed and the rubber pad grooved in, the effect of this process being to defy the efforts of the most restless animal to displace the pad. Mr. do Horsey claims and apparently with reason, th4t the rubber will entirely neutralise the possibility of the horse slipping, whether on greasy Cr frosty roads. Amongst the other.a.dventages said to be obtained in the use of this shoe is the prevention of con- cmsion to the tender part of the fo,t of the hlrse, thus making it of especial benefit to horses with very tender feet. Further advantages include the protection of the animals' feet from sharp stones and from the salt with which the streets are sometimes covered in winter. There is nothing whatever about the shoe to hurt the horse either in the fitting or wearing, as the hoof is no more affected than by the use of the ordinary shoe. Much commendation has already been bestowed upon the invention—for which a patent has been applied—by horse-shners and vetPrinarr snrenne. A specimen can 5-,:cu at eatablishment any hour dur- ing the day.

THPNGS HEARD AND SEEN

AT WAIRRING,TON.

Those who heardthe lecture by the Rev. Dinsdale T;Wung were highly delighted at the treat they had.. The rev. gentiknanhas a most racy and flow- ing style, and the way in which he sketched out the life of "Sammy Bradburn, the " Demosthenes of Methodism," was most interesting and entertain- ing. tie has a wondedui command of language,and he introduces into it just the right amount of humour to impart a piquancy to his lectures. The Young-x People's Society of Christian Endeavour have made an excellent commencement with their series-of lectures, and as so few other bodies have catered for the public- in a similar manner, they deserve every encouragement.

notice that the Vterary aryl Philosophical Society commence their session on Monday. As there are only six months in the session, and tbe meetings are held once a month.there are of course 'only- six rneetings4 but there has been as much variety prodded as could pos•ibly he obtained. STudents who are enthusiastic over the discoveries of the implements used by early man, will have an opportunity of both seeing and hearing about them on Monday,when Mr. Thomas ,May,F.E.I.S. will give a lecture upon the subject, and exhibit specimens. 'Electricians are catered for in November when Mr. C. Raily M.A., will lecture upon "The modern development of electric light and power." In December Mr. A. H, Crosfield will make his aud- ience Tong to be on a holiday to the Alps, by giving an account of his travels to those well-known moun- tains. Information of the English Guilds in the middle ages will be provided by the Rev. W. II: Dummond M.A., in January, and in February, the &en-dews-of the town will have their innings,when the Rev. Canon Dodd, M.A., will lecture upon " The literature of Queen Anne's reign." Dr. White M.R.C.S., will bring the series to a close in March, by discussing the question of the "-Migration of birds." The series of lectures promises to be very interesting and for the information of the general' publieit may be stated that the meetings are held' on the third Monday in each month at 7:30 p.m.

Warrington has been favoured this month by the. meeting of the Lancashire Association of Change ringers being held within its boreers. The study' of bell-ringing is no doubt interesting, but whron earth do they use such outlandish names for the ,ringing. To the uninitiated such terms as "Plain, Bob College Single," "Kent Treble Bob,"- "Canter- bury Pleasure Minor," and all other kinds of •"bobs' and " minors" are most mysterious terms, and whatever Bob has got to do with ringing, no- one but the bell-ringers know. Plowever,the chins- ing of church bells sounds very sweet, and we will forgive the ringers for the monstrous names,they give it.

Truancy does not seem to be on the decrease in Warrington, notwithstanding the strenuous efforts made by the school attendance officer. Either parents will not send their children to school Or when they do send them they are not particular as to whether they go there or not. On Tuesdvay at the police court a batch of three "- wailing wights" was sent to the "Clio" to be taught obedience. Tn half the cases a plea of illness ie put in by the parents and in the other half the children are sent but do not arrive. It was stated at the court that presently 70 lads would be leaving the ship and there would be plenty of room for aspirants, Let this be taken as a timely warning hy all youths who are addicted to the playful plan of playing " wag.*

MERLIN.

NEWTON DIVISION BOWLING ASSOCIATION.

The annual meeting held at tho close of the bowl- ing season, took place on Saturday evening at the Pied Bull Hotel, Newton. Mr. J. Liptrott presided and there was a full attendance of representatives from, the various clubs comprising the association. The chief business of the meeting was to cowider the rules, an important alteration being made therein, that, in place of playing in a matcb,as now, Sat home and 8 away, on the same day, it wa& pro- posed to play the full match on each others greens, in the same way as is adopted by the Lancashire and Cheshire Union, which was agreed to. There were one or two other alterations of a minor char- acter. It was stated that the Cup, had now been won outright by the North Ashton Club, and the question was raised as to whether Mr. lAgh would give another challenge cup for competition, but the matter was left Over for a week or two. Messrs. P. Garton and J. Ilill were appointed to audit the accounts,

LLANELLY, SOUTH WALES.

ASSAULT.—Peers Marsh, Haydock-street, was charged with having on the 28th September assaulted and beaten Henry Ashton, Buckley's-yard. The complainant stated that Marsh,who was drunk,was struggling with a young man named Cook on the date named. Witness told the defendant to let Cook alone, at which he struck witness three times. Corroborative evidence was given by Annie Naylor, Mr. A. Cook, and Mrs. Dickenson. The defen- dant denied assaulting the complainant. He was fined ss.and costs or in default seven days' imprisonment. He said he would go to gaol. CHARGE OF DESERTION.—Edward Stanley, 22,Howard- st., was charged with having on the Ist July deserted his wife Jane Isabel Stanley, living at 55, Foundry-st. The complainant stated that she was married to the de- fendant on the 6th January, 1894. On the Ist of July he told her to go to her mother's home and he would fol- low her. He never did so. The defendant never pro- vided any home, and they lived in lodgings for some time after they were married. Her husband had a pen- sion of 17s. 6d. per week. The defendant on being sworn stated that the woman deserted him, and he was living at the same house as when she left him. It was the third time she had left him, and on this last occasion she picked his pockets, and took everything belonging to him,even his shirts.—Mrs. Myers with whom the defend- ant lodged, also stated that the complainant left her husband voluntarily in July.—The magistrates dismissed the case. lief- Mr. J. THEFT FROM A CART.—William Corliss, lodging in Scotland-road was charged on remand with stealing a whip value is. 6d., the property of James Woodward, stall-keeper in the market house,on Wednesday evening. From Mr. Hunt's statement it appeared that on the date named Mr. Woodward's cart was standing in Corpora- tion-street and while there the prisoner stole the whip produced from it. He waa afterwards apprehended by D.S. Monaghan and brought to the police station. When charged with the theft he replied " 0 stealing eh ! " Mr. Woodward the complainant identified the whip and gave corroborrative evidence. The prisoner was fined 103. and costs or 14 days in goal. AGGRAVATED WIFE ASSAULT.—WiIIiam Baines a carter in the employ of Messrs. Pilkington was fined 103. and costs for assaulting his wile Elizabeth Baines, on the 3rd October.—Mrs. Baines, the complainant stated that on the date named the prisoner came home and charged her with being drunk and then took up a log of wood and struck her several times on the arm and shoulder with it. She was also kicked by him. For 18 years, complainant said, the prisoner had abused her and she was afraid of him. On Friday night he came home and threatened to murder her for getting a warrant against him.—Elizabeth Brookes, sister-in-law of the complainant stated that she was present on Friday night when the prisoner came home and using profane lan- guage threatened to murder his wife. Prisoner said he gave his wife L 1 a week regularly and that she neglected the children and was addicted to drink. He denied using profane language but did not say anything as to the assault.—Mr. Hunt spoke to the magistrates as to the prisoner's sobriety and steadiness. The chairman (Mr. J. Burgess) said that no doubt he had a lot to put up with but they would be compelled to fine him the above amount for the assault. DESERTION FROM THE RANKS. —A charge was preferred against John Taylor for deserting from the Durham Light Infantry, Newcastle-on-Tyne, in May last. He was remanded until Monday.

MONDAY.—Before Mr. F. Monks (chairman) Alderman Harrison Messrs. T. Birchall, W. Pierpoint, and S.

Wright. PRETENDING TO BE A DESERTER.—John Taylor was charged with feloniously pretending to be a deserter from Her Majesty's Durham Light Infantry. Inspector Spinks stated that the prisoner surrendered himself as a deserter to Constable Barnett and was brought before the magistrates, and remanded. On writing to the head quarters of the regiment the prisoner's statement was found to be untrue. A letter was read from the Regi- mental head quarters at Newcastle stating that the prisoner was discharged from the Durham Light Infan- try on the 17 April. He was committed to prison for seven days. ASSAULTING HIS WIFE. —George Maguire, labourer, 10, Stone-yard was charged with having on the sth inst. beaten and assaulted his wife Winifrid Maguire. Mrs. Maguire stated that her husband came home on the date named the worse for drink and gave her the black eye she exhibited in court. It was the second time during the week he had beaten her. lie was a very good hus- band when be kept free from drink and she did not wish him sent to prison. The defendant stated that hewas very sorry for what had occurred. It was all through drink. Alderman Harrison: It is all through your own stupidity in taking it (laughter). Defendant was dis- missed on payment of Costs. ANOTHER MA RKET THEFT. —Patrick Ryan was charf.-,ird with stealing from a stable in Golden Grove-st., on Saturday last, five bags of potatoes, value 124. 6d., the property of Alice Bailey. 20, Golden Grove-st. The de- fendant is a carter living at Perrin's-Court, and occa- sionally does odd jobs in the market. Between 11 and 12 on Saturday last the prisoner went to Bailey's house and took the stable key from the mantelpiece. He then proceeded to the stable and abstracted the potatoes. About two o'clock the same day the complainant missed the potatoes, and from some information she received she went to the prisoner's house and asked him about the goods. He was drunk and used bad language to her. On Monday she gage information to t'-ie police,and D.S.. Monaghan afterwards saw the prisoner,and brought him down to the Police Station and charged him with the theft, to which he gave the usual excuse of being drunk. Mrs. Bailey,the complainant, corroborated,and the prisoner was sent to gaol for one month's hard la- bour.

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

TUE BREAKING OF GAS LAMPS.--AN ANNUAL EXPENSE. At the Warrington Botough Court, on Monday,Arthur Richardson, Ackers pits ; Peter Boardman; Ackers Pits; Frank Watkins 9 Grappenhall-road ; Richard Hough, 555, Knutsford-road ; Herbert McQueen, 23, Locket- Street;:and James Young, Latchford Old Station were all charged with having on the 12th Septem'oer committed damage to the extent of 3s. to a gas lamp by breaking two panes of glass. Mr. J. L. Whittle town clerk prose- cuted, and stated that the Corporation were put to the expense of from L2O to £25 per year in repairing damaged lamp which was largely due to stone throw- ing. The defendants were brought there as a warning to others,and to put a stop to the wilful damage to lamps being done about the town. Mr. Heath gave evidence as to seeing the lads throwing at a gas lamp and doing the damage stated in Wash-lane, Latchford. The magis- trates severely reprimanded the lads, and discharged them, stating that if they came again they would be birched.

ARRESTED ON SUSPICION.

Joseph Minshull, who said he belonged to Little Leigh was charged at the Warrington Borough Court, on Mon- day, with being a suspected character, found on the enclosed premises of The Hollies, Latchford, at one o'clock that morning for some unlawful purpose.—P.C. Lewis stated that about one o'clock that morning he was on duty at Latchford and when near the "Cheshire Cheese "be heard some dogs barking. He went towards The Hollies-and stood at the ,gate listening when he thought he heard someone moving about. He went to the house and searched about but saw no one, but shortly afterwards he saw the prisoner lying awake under some shrubs. The man seemed as if he had had drink, and was recovering from the effects of it. Wit- ness brought him to the police station.—Mr. Broadbent stated that they could not find that anything had been stolen during the night.—The prisoner staid it was his first visit to Warrington and he went to the place where he was found to sleep.—He was fined ss. As YOUTHFUL DECEIVER. At the Warrington Borough Police Court, on Thurs- day morning,Jane Higgins (13) was charged with steal- ing from the store shed of Thomas Higgins, her uncle, situated at la. Stephen-st., on the 7th inst., a quantity of eggs,value £1 16s. ,Mr. Hunt stated that the defend- ant lives with her father, 18, Stephen-st., also the resi- dence of her uncle the complainant. On Saturday night the complainant locked up the store shed contain- ing the eggs,with a padlock and chain,the key for which was kept in the house. Early on Monday morning he noticed that the door of the store shed was open, and on going there he found the padlock and the eggs missing. Information was given to the police,and D.S. Monaghan ascertained on Wednesday that a girl had been seen selling eggs in Howley-Quay and Church-st.,on Sun- day and Monday. He afterwards apprehended the de- fendant at her home on Wednesday night.—The com- plainant stated that the defendant herself, was the first to call his attention to the theft, leading him to believe that she knew nothing at all about it.—Defendant, who pleaded guilty, said that another girl named Clara Wellington had helped her to steal the eggs : in fact,the accomplice,she said, had asked her to take them in order to settle a debt which Wellington's father had contracted. Defendant bad sold the eggs both on Sunday and Monday afterwards dividing the money with the other girl. D.S. Monaghan had made enquiries. but could not hear any- thing about the accomplice. Neither of the defendant's parents had appeared ,and it was stated that they did not know anything about the theft.—The magistrates re- manded the defendant until to-day, in order that the accomplice might be found and the girl's parents sum- moned to appear. OBSTRUCTION NEAR THE MARKET. At the Warrington Borough Court,on Monday.a num- ber of persons were charged with obstructing the thoroughfares near the Market Place. James Swales, a travelling hawker, was charged with allowing a horse and wagon to stand in the Market Place on the 26th ult., longer than necessary for loading and unloading. P.C. Delahey, who proved the case, stated that the horse and wagon stopped about 25 minutes nimnsite Mr. Car- lisle's shop. Defendant was fined 53.—A batch of similar cases was taken together, the defendants all beine, hawkers. Their names were John Baguley, Mill-street; James Ryan, Mill-st. ; Themas Carey, Old-road ; Wm. Comeford, Mill-lane ; and John Thomas Robinson, Town Hill. P.C. Delahey stated that on the 29th September he was near the Market in company with Constable Forster when he saw the defendants in charge of carts and handcarts. They stood in Market-st. and Lyme-st. for a considerable length of time, and when requested to move on they took little or no notice of witness or the assistant Market Inspector. On one occasion when the assistant Market Inspector asked them to move he was laughed at. Whenever they did move they simply changed places, and the street was greatly obstructed in consequence. They were repeatedly cautioned, but it had no effect whatever. Inspector Place gave evidence as to having previously warned the defendants Constable Forster corroborated P.C. Delahey's statement. Mr. Hunt stated that at Manchester a similar class of offenders were fined 403. each. They stopped people from going into the Market and were s great nuisance. It was really inciting to a breach of the peace for the police and officials to be laughed at, and taken no notice of.—The magistrates imposed a fine of 10s.and costs,or in defaq 34 days' imprisonment with hard labour.
