COWARDLY ASSAULT ON AWOMAN.—Benjamin Shepherd, who said he was a moulder at Dallam Forge, was charged with having on the previotis Wednesday, beaten and assaulted Bridget Griffin, 3, Bay Horse-yard. The com- plainant, an aged woman, stated that the prisoner came to her house on Wednesday night and asked could he stay there for the night. She refused to have him, whereupon he picked up a loaf of bread from the table and threw it at her, knocking her to the ground. He also picked up a teapot and threw the boiling contents over her arms. Three chairs and a table were broken by the prisoner, who was under the influence of drink at the time. Mrs. Ward, a neighbour. gave corroborative evidence. The prisoner's defence was that he had had a drop of beer. He was coaimitt,ed to prison for one month nard labour. CRUEL'TY TO A HORSE.—Joseph Rigby, Duke-street, Ditton. was charged with cruelly illtreating a horse by working it in an unfit state, and John Dudley,B, Thomas Buildings, Canal Bank, Chester, was charged with caus- ing it to be so worked. Sergeant Cheney repeated the evidence given at the Court the previous Friday when the case was adjourned, stating that the horse was very lame and unfit to travel to its destination at Chester. D.S. Monaghan also gave evidence. Inspector Luff, R.S.P.C.A., gave the result of his examination of the horse the following morning.. It wa.,, cruelty to travel the horse. Dudley was fined ss. and costs and Rigby was discharged.

- THE EXAMINER; SATURDAY, OCTOBER 13, 1894;

WARRINGTON OCCAIONAL COURT.

MONDAY.—Befere Mr. James Tinsley. CBARGE OF STEALING POTATOES.—CharIes Gathercole and' William Broad were charged with stealing 1301b5.01 potatoes, value 35., the property of Levi James, at Grap- penhall, on Sunday night. P.C. Brew met the men coming from Mr. James's field, and when they saw him they dropped the potatoes. He gave chase and caught them. A remand was granted to Daresbury Petty Sessions. THE ALLEGED TB EFT or FowLs.—On Wednesday be- fore Mr. James Tinsley, Joseph Robinson ,Latchford was charged with stealing three fowls, value 75., the pro- perty of Joseph Dunbobbin, Bridge Foot. Warrington,on the 3rd of October. Mr. Unsworth appeared for the nlaintiff.—The County Police Inspector said the defen- dant was brought up on the charge at the last Warring- ton County Potty Sessions. The police had not then had time to make inquiries into the matter, and the case was accordingly remanded, Robinson being admitted to bail. Since that time inquiries had been made, and the result was that the police did not offer any evidence against the defendant. He, therefore, asked his worship to dis- charge him.—Mr. Unsworth, who appeared for Robinson said he had come to the court prepared with evidence to rebut the story altogether. He was not aware until that morning that the police charge was to be withdrawn. He hoped his worship would allow him to express his suprise that such a charge had ever been preferred against his client, because so far as he could see there was not the slightest thread of evidence to justify the arrest.—Mr. Tinsley: I don't think it necessary to go on as the case is withdrawn.—Mr. Edgar (representing the magistrates' clerk) : There was evidence a week ago, which justified the magistrates in adjourning the case. The borough police apprehended the man.—Mr. Tinsleys The case is dismissed. There is an end of it.—Mr. Uris- worth : There is an end of the case, but I think it right to express my view of it—Mr. Tinsley: That is right as far as it goes.—Mr. Unsworth: The defendant has bcen charged with a serious offence.—Mr. Tinsley : The case is dismissed.

NEGLECT OF EDUCATION.—Joseph Basnett, Stone-street, was fined 61. and costs in two cases for not ensuring the regular attendance of his two children at school. DEPRAVITY.— A. frequent female visitor, Catherine Bowden, was sentenced to 14 days' imprisonment for soliciting in Mersey-st.. at 11.30 p.m. on the 6th inst. AN UNSUCCESSFUL APPLICATION. —Mr. G. W. Allen, solicitor. of Runcorn and Widnes, made an application on behalf of Daniel Ankers, labourer, to reduce a main- tenance order made upon him in favour of his wife. Mary Ankersof Christleton,near Chester. Mr.J.Burton, solicitor, Runcorn, appeared to oppose. Mr. Allen said that the order was made by a bench of Runcorn jus- tices in June last, when the present applicant was or- dered to pay 10s. per week towards the maintenance of his wife. He went on to say that his client's means were not what they were at the time the order was made. Ankers, while in the Army, had sustained certain in- juries to his hands through a gun falling upon him, and consequently could not earn as much as an ordinary man. He was discharged with a good character. Since the order was made he had tried to settle the unpleasant matter between himself and his wife, and she had pro- mised him that she would return and again live with him. She had now, however, without canse. broken her promise. Mr. Burton submitted that the means of the applicant had not altered since the order was made, and that there was no cause to reduce the allowance. Ankera could earn 15s. per week at the least as a la- bourer, and in addition to this, as an Army pensioner,he was in receipt of is. per day.—The application was dismissed.

THE SEERI3IP-CRYING NUISANCE IN RUNCORN ON SUN- DAYS.—John Findlow, 14 years of age, Cooper-street, was summoned for committing a nuisance in Ellesmere- st., Runcorn, by crying and exposing shrimps for sale, on the Lord's Day. Sergeant 0411 proved hearing the defendant shouting his fish in Ellesmere-st., near the Chapel, at about 2.45 in the afternoon of the previous Sunday. In reply to the Chairman the defendant said he was employed by a fisherman named White,who had not told him that he ought not to shout on Sundays. Superintendent Atherton explained that he had taken these proceedings in consequence of certain letters which he had received, and which had been published in the papers with reference to the nuisance caused by these boys shouting their goods on Sundays. The nui- sance was committed outside places of worship,and was the cause of much annoyance to the worshippers when engaged in their devotions. He had sent constables round to the various dealers in fish to warn them of the steps he intended taking in such cases as the present. He did not object to the peoplo going to the doors of the houses for the purpose of selling their fish ; but he did object to their shouting.—The Bench discharged the defendant, hut informed him that he, as well as other sellers of fish, would henceforth be liable to have their goods forfeited if they were discovered committing a similar nuisance.

Lord Roberts, affectionately alluded to by Tommy Atkins as " Litth Robs," is well under five feet six inches, but is remarkably muscular. Sir John Astley, the famous sporting enthusiast, died somewhat suddenly on Wednesday morning, at Park- place, St. James's-st., London.

RUGBY. WARRINGTON v. BROUGHTON RANGERS.

Warrington commenced their third League encounter on Saturday; before about 4,000 spectators, their op- ponents being the Rangers trom Broughton. As each team had had in successive weeks such dreadful trounc- ings at the hands of Oldham, it was a matter of curiosity how they would fare against each other. The Warring- ton team had been considerably changed from the one which met Oldham the previous week, for Boscow re- appeared after a long absence in his old place at full back, while Heesom was put in Carey's place at three- quarters, but as he could not play, Carey was called upon after all. Lawless's place was taken by Nevins whose place in the forwards was taken by Bailey. The following were the teams : Warrington: Full back, Boscow; three-quarters, Carey, Potts, Nevins and Cunningham; halt backs, James T. Bate and Jstin T. Bate; forwards, Fair. Turner, Jolley, Buckley, Thorniley, Taylor, Berry, and Dakin. Broughton Rangers : Full back, Hartley; three- quarters, Berry, Whitehead, Chapman, and Howe; half backs, Hosker and Garrity; forwards,. Medley, Skid- more, Shepherd, Oman, Campbell, Shaw, Fitton, and Clapham. Nevins kicked 4fr towards the pavilion goal, and his kick was returned to himself by Whitehead. The return kick was sent by Nevins in touch at the quarter flag, where a scrimmage succeeded the line out, which the forwards screwed finely taking play to the Rangers' line. Alter a short foray to the centre line, Carey returned to the attack by accepting a pass from " Smack " Bate. In- dividual runs were made by the Bates, Nevins, Turner, and Cunningham, and considerable ground gained, but their efforts were neutralised by similar efforts by Gar- rity, Chapman. and Berry. The latter was the first Ranger to reach the Warrington territory, and Boscow did well to bring him to earth. Encouraged by this success, Chapman and Berry pressed still further, but being too eager, the latter player sent too far, and Nevins touched down. A fine dribble by Turner and Jolley succeeded the drop out, and from a long throw out, Nevins ran to the line only to find Hartley in the way. The Rangers again broke away, but Boscow by a tremendous punt regained all the lost ground. At length Thorniley got over, but great was the disappointment when he was called back owing to the ball having been , in touch. The prolonged attack was at length cleared by the Broughton Berry, and passing to Chapman, the latter looked a certain scorer when he was grassed by Boscow. Boscow was again called upon to stop Berry, and at this point, the Rangers attacked strongly. On one occasion, Carey made a wretched pass which was intended for Cunningham, but which was secured by Whitehead, and had it not been for the superior speed of Cunningham, a score would certainly have been made. A combined dribble by Thorniley and Taylor took play to half way, where the movement was partaken in by Jolley, Dakin, and Turner, but the Rangers again came to the attack, only to gain another barren minor. Tay- lor's kick out was charged down by Hosker, and this was the means of still another minor being conceded. A good run by Nevins was spoiled by Jolley dallying with the pass, and Dakin also spoiled another pass by a knock forward. Uninteresting play followed and the Rangers gained their fourth minor. They had the best o the game to half time, but spoiled their chances by off side tackling, and the interval arrived with a clean sheet. After the interval the Rangers again pressed, and on one occasion off-side tackling by them, saved Warrington. The Rangers were repeatedly in front of the home goal during the first five minutes, and they were at length driven away by a dribble across the field by the for- wards. At this point the first really good passing was witnessed, in which the Bates, Carey, Potts, Cunning- ham, and Nevins took part, and a great amount of ground was gained in consequence. Dakin then had a dribble on his own account, and was very near getting over when Hartley staved off the danger. From the latter's kick, Boscow made his mark in a good position, and Nevins made a good attempt at the place, but only a minor resulted. There was now a formidable attack on the part of the Rangers, ard Berry (Broughton) secured within a yard off the line with no one to stop him. Great was the chagrin of the Rangers when he lost possession, and a scrimmage was ordered, which was well rushed by the Warrington forwards. Howe now made a brilliant run, but as he was not supported, he did not prove dangerous thanks to Cunningham and Boscow. Potts roused hopes by a dash through his oppoisents, but lacking sufficient speed, his effort failed. Everyone was now making up their mind that the game would end in a pointless draw,when suddenly " Smack " Bate passed to Jack Bate, and the latter, passing all op- position, got over the line amid loud cheers. Taylor was not successful in increasing the lead. The Rangers we only dangerous once after this, when Boscow stopped an almost certain try, and the Warrington for- wards possessing superior stamina, held the opposing forces in check until the end of the game, but as they could not score again, the result was a victory for War- rington by a try and two minors to four minors. WARRINGTON " A " v. BROUGHTON "A." The Warrington " A " team gained their first two points on Saturday in the Lancashire Club Champioe- ship competition when they defeated the Broughton Rangers " A " team at Broughton. It was the first engagement of the season for the home team, and amongst their fifteen was included Nicholas, a well- known runner, and at one time a player in the Salford team. There was not a very large attendance when the ball was set in motion. For a short time play raged in Warrington quarters and on one or two occasions the home team became dangerous, but were unable to score. Altar a number of scrimmages Warrington broke away and took play into Broughton territory. Here Foden got possession of the ball, and after a dodgy run near to the Broughton line, he passed to Thornton who scored a try in a favourable position. Lawless took the place kick, and was successful in shooting the ball over the bar. The home team made strong efforts to equalize but the Warrington defence was impassable. As the game progressed Warrington improved in their play and several times entered the home quarters, but without further scoring. The whistle for half-time sounded with Warrington leading by a goal to nil. In the second half of the game a good deal of unseemly behaviour on the part of the spectators was noticeable. The Warrington touch-judge's decisions we re received with hooting, and some of the spectators so far overstepped their discretion as to throw at hire, conduct which the referee was not slow to condemn. Warrington by far had the best of the game in the second half and only twice were the home team dangerous. Lawless further augmented the Warrington score by a splendidly dropped goal. This was followed soon afterwards by Nicholas intercepting a pass and racing over the Warrington line. The same player also succeeded at the goal kick to the intense delight of the spectators. The remainder of the game was in favour of Warrington who retired victors of a, well-fought game by two goals to one coal. The follow- ing were the teams :—Warrington : Mannion, fnll-back ; Thornton, Lawless, Gibson, Edelsten, three-quarter backs ; Forster and Foden, halfbacks; Randles,Burrows, Wilson, Fairhurst, Savage, Andrews, Houghton and Barratt, forwards ; Browghton Rangers : W. Emethurst, fell back ; H. Bentley, E. Anderson, G. Walsh, T. L. Nicholas, three-quarter backs ; FL Allcock, and W.Lead-

better, half-backs; C. Stansfield, G. Arrowsmith, G. Worrall, J. Fletcher, W. Brcome, J. Connor, J. Irlam, and W. Hilton, forwards.

RUNCORN RECREATION V. SALE ASHFIELD.—This match was played at Sale on Saturday afternoon. The first half was splendidly contested, the Sale men having a slight advantage, but could not do anything against the visitors' defence, while Minshull succeeded in scoring 3 try for the Recs., at which the score stpod at the interval. In the second portion of the game the Runcorn men played up grandly, and from a penalty Clay kicked a splendid goal. The game all through had been played at high pressure, and at times was very exciting. Just before the close W. Gregory signalizing his appearance in the team by running in a try near the corner,the game ending in a win for the Recreation by one (penalty) goal, two tries, to nothing.

WARRINGTON EXCELHORS V. LEESFIELD TRINITY.— These smart Oldham juniors paid their first visit to Wilderspool Causeway to try conclusions with the Ex- celsiors. The late arrival of the visitors prevented the kick off till 4.30, when the home team started, and for a few minutes play was chiefly in midfield. A rush by the visitors' forwards looked dangerous, but Hockenhull got the oval away nicely. The home team now had a turn, and from some loose work had hard lines, Brob- bin, Hughes,and Royle being prominent. This was fol- lowed by a capital effort by Hockenhull, who put in a neat run and kick. However, they dribbled the leather down the field, and a mistake by Sparks let in Woods, who scored an easy try. The place kick proved a failure, which brought the interval,the visitors leading by a try. Play in the other half calls for no comment, but it left the visitors winners by a try to nil. ST. PETERS V. WIDNES JOCKS IsT.—This match .was played on the ground of the former. The home captain winning the toss the visitors kicked off into the homes- ters quarters. Time arrived the score being :—St. Peters, 2 goals,l try, 2 miners; Widnes Jocks, 1 minor. WARRINGTON RANGERS V. CHESTER COLLEGE.—PIayed at Lovely-lane on Saturday. The Rangers kicked off the ball being returned to Jackson who ran and passed to Gleave, that player sending the ball into touch near the line. From the first scrimmage formed, Hinchliffe got hold and feigning to pass slipped through his op- ponents and scored a clever try, Gleave from the place landing a fine goal. Elated by this early success, the homesters played up with renewed energy but over eagerness spoiled several fine openings. Eventually Chester had a look in,smart play by Butterworth putting the hotnesters on the defensive and a miskick by the Rangers' back let in Cobb, the goal kick being at fault. The game hereabouts became very interesting,each side attacking in turn, but nothing further was recorded when half time was called with the score, Rangers one goal ; Chester one try. After the interval the College forwards went away with great dash, but the home defence left nothing to be desired, and play continued to be very even until Railton for the visitors got possession and running strongly got over near the corner. The Rangers being now in a minority strove hard to equalise and a splendid piece of passing along the line of backs saw Price romping over in fine style. Gleave however failed to augment, From the drop out the Rangers con- tinued to hold the upper hand and finally retired victors by one goal, one try to Chester College two tries.

WARRINGTON ST. MARY'S v. STOCKTON HEATH.—The above teams met on Saturday on the Liverpool-rd ground before 1,000 spectators. The "Heathens' won the toss, and elected to play with the wind. G.Hampsen started the ball for the Saints, Broady making a good return. The Saints were soon pressing Stockton Heath on their lines, when Cross was forced to kick dead. Stockton Heath now had a turn,the forwards taking the ball down the field, only to see Roughley quite safe. Some good play by Barber and Webster transferred play to the Saints" lines, where the homesters were penalized for picking out of the scrimmage. Broady took the place kick, but failed to enhance its value. Smart kicking by F. Hampson and Higginbottom improved the Saints' position, and a moment later a splendid long kick by Burton saw the Eleathens defending. After series of scrimmages the Saints began to show the advantage.and this they maintained almost continuously throughout the game, when from a scrimmage Lally secured, but was grassed by Burton when looking dangerous. Al- though the visitors screwed the scrimmages the defence could not be penetrated. Half time arrived with the score nil. On resuming, the Saints pressed and forced the Heathens to defend, when Barber gathered and passed to Webster, but the latter was upset by Warbur- ton before he had gone far. St. Mary's now brightened up considerably, and for the remainder of the second half had much the best of the game. A line out gave them an advantage, and they conceded a minor. From the drop out the Saints again got possession, and were making nicely fo r the line when the referee recalled them for some informality. Several scrimmages ensued and a combined rush by the Stockton Heath forwards forced Burrows to save. From a scrimmage the home- sters' forwards came on with a rush, and from some scrambling play Monks scored, Burton being responsible for the goal. From the drop out, Thomas n.ade a good return, forcing Stockton Heath to defend again. G. Hampson put in some capital play and did splendid service for his team. The Saints eventually ran out victors of a hard fought gameby 1 goal to nil. RUNCORN v. HUNSLET.—The senior Runcorn team journeyed to Hunslet for the first time on Saturday, to engage the champions of this town. The match took place on the Parkside enclosure, the following being the respective teams :—Runcorn: Houghton, back; Richard- son, Warder, Butterworth and Gayter, three-quarter hacks; Robinson and J. Faulkner, half-backs; Taylor, Farmer, Reece, Johnson, Pendlebury, Davies; Langley and Lightfoot, forwards. Hunslet : Robinson. back ; Mitchell, A. Goldthorpe, W. Goldthorpe and Wright, three-quarter hacks; Townsend and Simpson. half- backs; Kaye, Walsh, Rathmell, Beacon, Cooper, Manson, Rubry and Ducker forwards. Runcorn kicked off in a drizzling rain and play remained for sometime in mid field. The homesters got away at length and after some very tough play Rubry got over at the corner. Goldthorpe failed to improve. Later on Run- corn, who had shown some very good pass- ing equalized. Give and take play followed ' until Hunslet were penalized and the Runcorn lads dashed away and secured a second try, Butterworth making a poor attempt to secure a major point. The homesters now made the Runcornians turn tail and defend their own territory. Houghton repelled some splendid shots, and tackled well. Brilliant open play by the Runcurn forwards succeeded, but Hunslet kept the ball tight in the packs, and went in for the close rushing game, trusting to their strength and weight. Townsend eventually scored at the corner, no goal resulting from the place. The whistle sounded for the interval with the scores even, two tries each. The second portion of the game was very rough and attended with more casualities than the first portion. Runcorn behaved pluckily, and gave their opponents considerable trouble before they were able to secure a goal. This success on the part of the homesters spurred the visitors on and they showed capital form in their endeavours to make matters even. In this they could not succeed, however, and the game, which had proved a very rough one,ended with a win for Hunslet by one goal two tries to Runcorn two tries.

ORFORD CRESCENT V. PARR VICTORIA (Senior League). —This match was played on the Crescent ground, on Saturday, Mr. Dearden, Earlestown, being the referee. The Vies, won the toss and kicking down hill to the rail- way end, Wilson set the ball in motion. From the com- mencement the Crescent forwards pressed. J.Weston and E. Mather, wings centreing grand, but owing to lack of shooting only gal kicks resulted. At last the visitors kicked the ball down the field and Jones thinking Hayes was going to take the kick left it for him only to see one of the Vies, men put a very simple goal, Hayes clearitag but the Vies, forwards were too sharp and rushed the only point of the day. The Crescent team attacked and the goal keeper took the ball under the bar,but the referee didn't seem to think it a goal. From the restart to finish both teams tried the best, especially the visitors, but they always found Hayes with open arms, glad to receive any shots which were occasionally sent in. When whistle was blown Parr were the victors by 1 goal to nil.—For the homesters Hayes played grand in goal and saved his club time after time, as is his usual form. J. Jones and Dutton playing grand at backs and Thompson was the only half worth mentioning, his defence being excellent. All the forwards played good combination but are poor at shooting. The visitors played a good game throughout, but the Crescent were the best club of the day. LANCASHIRE ALLIANCE.—EARLESTOWN V. HATDOCIC.— Earlestown journeyed to Haydock on Saturday after- noon to meet the Alliance cup-holders. This is Earles- town's first season in the Alliance, and as they have worked their way into a good position, much interest was evinced in their meeting with Haydock, conse- quently a very large number of their supporters wended their way to Haydock to witness the struggle, and they were rewarded by an excellent contest, both sides put- ting forth every effort to gain the premier point. Both teams were fully represented, and Earlestown winning the toss, Fielding kicked off for Haydock before about 3-,000 spectators. Earlestown at once took up the attack, and kept Haydock in their own quarters for some time. Getting an opportunity, Conway shot in but Lee saved. After a free kick to Haydock, Lee and Conway with some tricky play worked the leather well down ,and Con- way centreing, Siddeley headed the ball over. Haydock now made a visit to the Earlestown goal, but Peat cleared and again the bale was worked down to the wagon goal, but the final shot went over. Some even play ensued, but the pace being exceedingly fast, no judgment in shooting was exercised, and there was a tendency to play the man instead cf the ball. Haydock attacked and worked the ball to the Earlestown quar- ters, but the final shot was futile. A corner fell to Hay- dock which they failed to improve upon, and the visi- tors worked the ball down to the home goal, Siddeley, Lee, and Conway being noticeable for a smart bit of head play. Play still continued in the Haydock quarters. but Earlestown were unable to break through the defence of Greenhalgh and Wainwright. Another assault was made on the Haydock goal, but Greenhalgh cleared. Hands off Preston gave Earlestown a chance, but the ball was cleared, and Haydock getting hold raced away, but the ball was worked out. Siddeley got possession and cleverly worked the globe down, but his final shot went wide. Haydock now visited the Earlestown but were unable to lower their colours. A series of 7 kicks brought the ball down to the Haydock goal, and Lee and Conway after a pretty bit of play placed the Haydock goal in danger, but unfortunately the ball went over. Again they attacked the home goal, but experi- enced hard, luck in not scoring. Haydock now worked up, and had their first shot at goal which was taken by Fielding, but it went wide of the mark. Earlestown again pressed and Lee shot, but the ball went over the line. Haydock now got hold, and Worsley and Fielding worked up, Worsley shot, and scored for Haydock. From the kick-off, they again made an attack on the Earlestown goal, but fsiled to score, and half time was reached with the score as above. On turning round, Earlestown rushed up, and from a scrimmage in front of goal, Harrison headed through his own goal, thus equalising. Haydock now ran down, and Fielding shot, but the ball hit the post and went out. Haydock now pressed a little, but the Earlestown backs were equal to all attempts to score. Earlestown attacked, and Jenkin- son headed, but the ball just passed out. A visit was made to the Earlestown goal, but the final shot was wide. A corner fell to Haydock, but the ball was got away. Earlestown bombarded the Haydock goal for some.time, and had several near chances of scoring, but they experi- enced hard luck. Play became of a give and take char- acter, until Earlestown getting hold, and Siddeley made a splendid effort, but the ball went out. Again they attacked and Conway getting the ball at his toes narrowly missed scoring. A corner fell to Haydock,but it was fruitless, Siddeley played a grand game hereabouts, and put in some nice shots. A corner to Earlestown was nicely placed, but it waa headed over the bar. Play became pretty even, both sides attacking, but the game ended in a draw of a goal each. Hay. dock: Goal, Lee; backs, Greenhalgh and Wainwright; half backs, Wilson, Harrison and Preston ; forwards, Meyer, Morris, Fielding, Shaw and Worsley.

BEWSEY ALL SAINTS V. HOLY TRINITY S.S.—This match was played on the ground of the former on Saturday and resulted in a win for the saints by one to nil, the saints playing a rough game throughout. Jones, the Trinity full back, retired hurt, and it was during his absence that Bewsey secored their winning point.

WARRINGTON V. LEEK.—This match was played at Warrington on Saturday before a poor "gate." William- son kicked off for Warrington, and the ball was well re- turned by the visiting backs. Rae stopped a nice rush by the Leek men, and Reid and Mather sent in several long kicks. Leek then made an invasion of the War- rington half, and Crosby defeated Williamson and scored the first goal for Leek. The visiting team pressed the remainder of the half, Heywood shooting another goal before half-time was called, when the score stood Leek two goals to nil. Warrington pressed through the first portion of the second half, and Mather scored for the homesters. In the latter stages Leek again had the t'est of matters. Williamson had to save time after time,and at last failed to stop a rush of the forwards,which added another goal to the visitors' score. Time was called with the score in favour of Leek by three goals to one.

WARRINGTON ST. ELPHIN'S V. MANCHESTER WAN- DERERS.—The above teams met at Old Trafford on Satur- day. The visitors only took ten men, owing to the in- disposition of Phcenix,who cried off at the last moment. Marshall won the toss, and the home centre kicked off. Nothing was scored till near half time, when Marshall managed to get one past the home custodian. Half time was called with the score St.Elphin's one goal,Wanderers nil. Immediately after the restart Marshall registered his second with a low, swift shot. This reverse stirred up the home forwards, who went off with great dash, and using their superior weight rushed the ball past Robson. Hatton raceti down in fine style and, though hemmed in at the corner he managed to pass back to Tickell, who scored with a fine long shot. In a short time the Saints' forwards were up again and Hickin- bottom crossed to Dendy, who scored the fourth. The home forwards then went off with another burst and, profiting by a misunderstanding on the part of one of the backs, scored a second goal. Soon after Hickin- bottom was enabled to get up and score a fifth. Shortly before time the Wanderers got through andscored again. The signal for ceasing play was then given with the score St..Elphins, five goals ; Wanderers, three.

ANOTHER SEAMAN DROWNED AT RUNCORN.

THE SAD END OF A NIGHT'S PLEASURE.

An inquiry was made on Monday afternoon at the C- netery offices, Greenway-road, by Mr. J. E. Worsley, coroner, relative to the demise of Peter Traynor, 21 years of age,a seaman,of Liverpool,who was drowned on Friday night, by falling into the Ship Canal at the Old Quay, Runcorn.—Captain Easeley, attended on behalf of the Ship Canal Comoany.—The body was identified by Michael Duffy, fish dealer. 75, Kensington, Liverpool, as that of his cousin, Peter Traynor, whom he had last seen three months ago.— From the testimony of Henry Traynor,an "able-bodied" on board the s.s. "Trieste," and a resident of Liver- Pool, it is apparent that the deceased, who was also an " A .B." on board the same vessel, left the ship about eight o'clock on Friday night,in the company of witness and another man named Thomas Beahan, the ship's carpenter. The ship, which was loaded with timber and bound for Manchester, was moored against the Old Quay Dock, Runcorn. Tim three men went into the town and each one had about four pints of beer before they thought of retarning at 10.30. They started in the direction of tke ship, all of them being perfectly aware of what they were doing. On , reaching another public- house Peter Traynor wished,tti go in to get more drink, but the other two men peritiaded him not to go,the wit- ness saying to the deceased at the time that the road to their ship was a very dangerOus one, and that they would have to be very careful. They all went on again, and when they got to the lock, over which they had to pass, the carpenter walked in advance, deceased went next, and witness followed about ten yards behind. Whilst witness was singing Peter Traynor suddenly dis- appeared into the lock. Instantly witness looked over the lock gate, but the night was so dark that nothing could be seen. He then ran to the watchman's hut and told him of the occurrence. He then snatched up a life buoy, returned to the lock,and threw it into the water. He could not see the deceased, but he threw the buoy where he thought the deceased might have been. The watchman took a boat round and afterwards entered the lock. When the body was found at two o'clock the following morning witness was on board his ship. The decoased and the carpenter were both on the best of terms, and the fact that he was absent from the inquiry did not create any suspicions in witness's rnind.—Edwm Caunce, lock keeper, testified to recovering the body at two o'clock on Saturday morning. It was very dark on Friday night. He did not hear TrayKor fall into the lock, but he heard some one shout out "Peter." He saw the last witness throw the life buoy into the lock, but owing to the darkness they could not discern the whereabouts of deceased. The water was about 14 feet deep where the man fell in. Witness did not hear any noise on the night of the occurrence which would lead him to thick that the men had been quarrelling.—The Coroner, in. reviewing the evidence, said he thought that everything possible had been done, for the deceased un- der the circumstances, and advised that a verdist of "Accidental death'? be returned.—The jury recorded this verdict. •

PROPOSED SMALLPDX HOSPITAL FOR

WARRINGTON.

In consequence of a proposal made by the War- rington Corporation to the County Council that a smallpox hospital should be provided for Warring- ton and the adjoining districts, to be maiatained At the joint expense of the authorities of those dis- tricts, a conference was held at the county offices at Preston on Friday afternoon. The conferenge was attended by members of the different authori- ties interested. Warrington Corporation was re- presented by Aldermen Sutton and Blackhurst, and Councillor Skelton, (members of the Health Cora- mittee,) the Town Clerk (Mr. J. Lyon Whittle), and Dr. Gornall (medical officer); Warrington SanitarY Authority by Rev. G. F. Roberts, Mr. A. Jolley, Mr. S. Sutton (clerk), and Dr. Sephton (medical officer); Newton Commissioners by Messrs. Ormand and Ratcliffe; and Leigh Local Board by Mr. Williams (clerk), Mr. Fairclough, and an: other member. Alderman Sutton was voted to the chair, and Dr. Sergeant, county medical officer, explained the object of conference. It was proposed to constitute the unions of War' rington and Leigh into a joint hospital district foro the treatment of smallpox, the maintenance 0' which will be made by a rateable contribution frona each of the authorities, and the expenses of the patients borne in proportion to the number sent from each authority. After a somewhat lengthY discussion in which most present took part, a 80b committee was appointed to report upon the con- stitution of the proposed hospital committee, the contribution of the expenses, and the most suitable site for the smallpox hospital available for the whole of the district. A resolution was passed pa the proposition of Councillor Skelton that in the opinion of the conference, it was desirable that Oa local authoritieswith in the unions of Warrington and Leigh be constituted into a joint hospital district under the Isolation Hospitals Act,1893, for the pa' pose of dealing with cases of smallpox. The con- ference was adjourned sine die to await the report of the sub-committee which it is expected will be given during next month.

On a downy couch they sat together, The lamplight burning low and dim ; The old Wks had retired to slumber— 'Twas eleven-forty-five P.M. Their tales of love and sweet canoodling, Ten seconds they'd left off or so ; Their hearts felt poised 'tween love and sadness, Because—twas time that he should go. He fidgeted, in mild distraction, As if he'd something in his mind He lacked the enterprise to utter, Like a lump of lead tossed in the wind ! At last he mustered up some courage, And said, " my Sweetest Gwendoline, I'm to request a little favour Which you I trust will not decline." She blushed a sea-green jet carnation, That almost rivalled Sankey Brook, Or Mersey's pale pellucid waters, Where ardeec anglers " fish for fluke"— And said, in love-struck agitation, "My dearest Adam, well know you There's nought within my power, in reason, For you I would not gladly do." " Thanks,thanks, my precious rose-eyed darling' My trouble is like this, you see : I know the big black dog so savage, Out on the lawn's awaiting me. WARRINGTON RURAL SANITARY A-UTHORITY.

The monthly meeting of the above Authority was held at the Board Room of the Work h )use on Fri- day. The Rev. G. F. Roberts presided and there were also present Messrs. W. Long, J.P., W. Hough- ton, Dignum, R. Heaton, A. Jolley, G. Ratcliffe, S. Sutton (clerk), W. Owen (surveyor), F. Kerfoot (nuisance inspector), and Dr. Sephton (medical sofficer).

DISPUTED PLANS.

_ The CLERK said that Mr. Gough was in attendance With reference to the dispute about the plans of five houses which he was building at Sankey, and which Mr. Owen had not passed.—Mr. OWEN said that before Mr. Gough came into the room, he would like to say that he refused to pass the plans because the drains were not satisfactory, and the water also. The analysis of the water was very unsatisfactory, and the drainage in the course which it was proposed to take would affect water used by cattle for drinking purposes. So long as plans were in accordance with the bye-laws, he had no other course but to pass them. The plans of Mr. Gough were in accordance with the bve-laws, and he had therefore no course but to pass 'them. He (Mr. Owen) had received a letter from Mr. Jeans, the solicitor of Mr. 'Gough, stating that the water was not used by cattle, and that if the Authority did not pass the plans, he :would apply to the Queen's Bench for a mandamus. His , information was that the water was used for cattle. His , Opinion was that the best plan would be to pass the plans and to endorse it to the effect that the drainage must not vitiate the water and take no further course until Mr. Gough applied for a certificate of habitation.—Mr. Gough was then called in and said that his clans were in accordance with the bye-laws. He denied that the drains polluted the water, and said that the drains of Several other houses went into the same brook. He also alleged that the drains were in use previously. Be 'complained that there was a movement against his building the houses because the property Was on a lower scale than the other buildings in the district, and that they were near to a stack yard.—Mr. OWE,N said that the drain had never previously been used by five houses, and said that it would pollute the water. The CRAIRMAN said that the Authority were determined to Prevent the pollution of streams, and they were most careful in all new cases to prevent such taking place.— Alter further discussion, the Authority passed the plans .and endorsed them according to the advice of Mr. Owen.

RIXTON BURIAL GROUND.

The CLERK announced that a satisfactory agreement had been come to to all parties with reference to the Rixton burial around, by a portion of the unallotted ground being apportioned to the Roman Catholics. MEDICAL OFFICER'S REPORT.

Dr SEPIITON reported that the births during the month Lad been 40, deaths 10. The death rate was equal to 86 per 1,000 against 129 during the last three months, and 23-3 the corresponding month last year. The death rate from zymotic diseases had been 1.4 per 1,000, against 1•2 the last three months. and 5.2 the corres- Donding month of last year. Warrington, October 10th, 1894.

THE ELECTIONS AT EARLESTOWN. Sir,—As the time draws near when our Parish Counci will have to be elected, we are unexpectedly favoured with quite a plethora of candidates for the honour of representing us, and it is suggested by way of thinning them to apply a test question. One proposed 4with all due gravity is the old open question of the common lands. It is affirmed that the uneu- closed land forming part of the Newton Com- mon belongs by right to the inhabitants of this parish, and it may assist these candidates, who mostly are new men, if i state the circumstances of the case as nearly as is at present known. During the last dozen or score of years some hundred houses have been built at the bottom of the Common near the Race Course and the highway. which was for- merly part of the Bolton Trust. This road was handed over to the Newton Improvement Commissioners on the expiry of that Trust, and they forthwith set up a couple of stones to mark the exact width of the road of which they were then in possession with the WIIiIIOUS :initials N.I.C. They also passed plan after plan as the afore said houses were built. But when a set of plans for houses on the opposite. and as yet unoccupied side of the road, were presented to them for approval they deferred its consideration and finally left toe would-be builder to wait till the new Council come into office. The question in my mind is this—lid the Lord of the Manor, who is also owner of nearly all the land, grant this person a lease, or was the application merely

tentative. It snay be information to know that as the law stands the Lord of the Manor must in any case take all below, even if the inhabitants could make good their claim to what appears on the surface of this not inconsiderable piece of ground. And that he is Lord of the Manor is a fact notto be controverted, for as long as Newton Fair lasted under the old regime the fact was dinned into our ears. Remembering then that there are only two free- holders in the parisn besides the Dean School Trust, which is a corporete bodrunder Government control, and that all the other freeholders have been brought out by the predecessors in title of the Lord of the Manor during the last 300 years, before which time this family had no possession this side the Sankey Brook, the question then is, " Whom doee the remainder of the land go to? If to the inhabitants, then to what inhabit- ants ? for if I mistake not the ancient parish was the palish of Winwick which extended from the Alt (near Altcar) to the Glaz,,some few miles beyond Warrington." " Did not I call thee to curse and lo.tliou hest altogether blessed mine enemy." Which were the more assinine performance, to go to law without money or without knowledge? As regards the Dean Sehool Trust, this property which is in.lands on the opposite side of the race course was exchanged by a former Lord of the Manor in order to consolidate his estate near the Dean Dam, and whatever' his rights in that direction then were, they were recog- nized by the Legislature and there is no getting beyond them; moreover the arrangement is highly profitable to the inhabitants of this same parish et Nee t m—and even more so since the re-settlement which took place. some years ago, and which in a humble way I had some- thing to do with; and what makes me remember it so well was that I had along with another.now deceased, the pleasure of paying my own law expenses, and in due time was quite forgotten by this highly appreciative, and generous public:, as 'a penalty for interfering with their honour's pleasure. Tnis was a fact not a fiction. But as regards this piece of land in question of which the very authorities seem to be in utter darkness—who gave leatte ts dig that great pit in it which in imagina- tion some see transformed into a beautiful artificial lake —and what became of the clay or bricks? Let us also imagine that they were built into the Nine Arches which we see close by and remember that the Lord of the Manor of toe date when that was built was very much ahead of some of his neighbours and exercised these acts of owner- ship with a purpose. I hear some one say covertly, " What does he know about such a matter ?" Only this. I was present on Lindrick Common when the people of Sheffield contested the right to enclose it, and followed Sir William Lena, as speaker on that occasion, and advised them to stick to any rights they might have in the Common. But as it turned out every freeholder had been brought out and there waa no landholder to propitiate, so the lawless, nameless men counted for nothing. But if the people think they can make good this contention, then I can only repeat, try it, bat Tam not aware that the law has been yet altered to give mere leaseholders any rights, for this falls tar short of a freehold. Sir William Leng said no, no, to what I said, but quietly let the whole matter slide after making us quite a holiday and picnic on the Common, which very much reminded me of Newton Common, and of which I did not fail to tell them. This will be very unpalatable, and make some people pull wry faces, but facts must be faced by public men aspiring to lead others. But enough; we want plenty of workingmen on our councils, but not merely workingmen, they must be men of equal capacity with others and not the naere.echo of their social superiors, not ot the mere builder man- kind who may or may not be as substantial as his new cast-iron brick built houses. And the questions are still the old ones of peace between rival factions, retrench- ment and ieform,none of which have as yet been mooted —the candidates being so busy with their own personal advancement that they have not as yet had time to tell us why we should prefer them to the "old gang." Where we shall end I can scarely see; for some have been asked to stand who are on no list whatever, but as the Act says anyone can stand, the presumption is that anyone will do who is not troubled with bashfulness, or who has a good opinion of his own merits. Most fortun- ately this approaching election is by ballot, and the people are gradually learning how to use it. Nor are we tied down to anyone's nominees. Divide and conquer is still a good motto and if that will not do in all cases then we must sacrifice a few votes and plump for the best men.—Yours faithfully, Crow-lane, Newton-le-Willows.

P. M. CAMPBELL.

Coming to the players, the first place should be given to Joe Boscow, who played as well as he has ever done. Ile had plenty of work to do, and he came out with fly- ing colours. His admirers were delighted with his play, and it is probable that he will become a fixture upon the team. It will be seen that Warrington art still fourth in the list, and:are likely to continuelso or thereabouts to-day.

To-day the team will meet St. Helens at St. Helens. They will have to put their best foot first to come out on the right side, as the Saints inflicted a crushing defeat on Tyldesley last Saturday. Of course this is not very surprising as St. Helens have a special, thirst for the Tyldesley men's gore. Warrington have only one department in which they are really very deficient, and that is the three quarters. If they could only get a strong set, they would give a good account of themselves against any team,

The following is the team which is selected to with- stand the onslaughts of the St. Helens team to-day : full back, J3oscow ; three-quarters, Potts, Heesom, Nevins, and Cunningham ; half-backs, James T. Bate, and John T. Bate; forwards, Fair Turner, Jolley, Thornily,Taylor, Berry, Dakin, Bailey, and Buckley.

I notice that for the trial match for Lancashire County players, known as Whites v. Stripes, which will take place at Manchester on Wednesday, Heesom is reserve for fall-back, and Taylor for forward, This is a long way from being chosen as county pl4ersl but still they are " gettin' on."

The "A" team on Saturday stemmed the tide of losses and draws, which have been, too Irequent with them this season, by defeating Broughton Rangers "A" team on their own graund. The match was an evenly- contested one, and had it not been for the unpleasant- ness caused by the spectators in being too ready to ex- press their biassed opinions on the judgments of the [ touch judge for Warrington,a most pleasant afternoon's ' play would hr ve been witnessed. As it was, hooting and throwing of dirt were indulged in by the spectators' who seemed to dislike seeing their team losing. If such treatment is often accorded to visitors the committee May in future have some difficulty in obtaining fixtures. Lawless was the most successful scorer in the Warring- ton team, his dropped goal proving the winning point in the game. For the homesters Nicholas was the only one to score. To-day the "A" team play on their native heath, when their opponents will be the" A" team from St. Helens, and if the play pf" the Warrington team is equal to last Saturday, they should be able to add a further two" points in their favour in tbo .I,,tague gham- pionship.

A match which was Worth seeing.was thoo.'t gatur- daY between Stockton Heath and St. Mary's . played in a field off Liverpool-road, on Saturday. For Borne time the Stockton Heath players had expressed their intention to wipe the door with the St.-Mary:.s playe,ra, while the latter were filled with a similar amiable desire to do the same with the "heathens." There was consequently a tremendous attenclance—for a junior match—to F which would carry out their intentions. The Stock_ Heath team were generally allowed to have the best chance as they have captured so many ex-Warringtonians but the St. Mary's team worked with a will and while preventing a score being made against them,they by the efforts of Monks got over, and Burton's foot gave a lead of five points. This is a feather in the cap of St. Marv's, for to beat a team which possesses such men as Webster. Barber, Broady, Cross, and Lally is no mean task. Won't there be a 'gate' at the return match? The. Warrington Rangers who with the two teams aboreanentioned, are without doubt the leading junior Rugby club in the town also gained a victory at the expense of Chester College. The victory was however not a very decisive one for it was by the goal kick, the final result being Rangers a goal and a try to two tries.

MR. CHAMBERLAIN'S PROGRAMME.

Mr. J. Chamberlain, MP., addressing his constituents in Birmingham on Thursday night, said for once he in- tended to leave the Irish qqestion severely alone, especi- ally as the baneful agitation which for so many years disturbed the peace ri the 'country had now spent its force. The interests of Great Britian had been neglected simply because the Government held its life entirely at the mercy of its Irish allies. It was time that the people .of this country devdted themselves to pressing social questions. The most important, he considered, was that of temperance. In the past,attempts had been made rather to punish the publican than to reclaim the drunk- ard. Hn believed that the Local Veto Bill was a had because it really was a bill for restricting local op- tion. It was also an upiust bill, bemuse it proposed to do good by doing evil. by taking away the legal property of the publicans, by destroying their livelihood. It was, further, an unequal and an impracticable bill: On this question of temper- ance he was in entire agreement with Mr. Gladstone. The past policy had been an impospare. The only safe principle on Which they notild act was to take the control of the trade into publc hands and eliminate personal gain from it, btit they must ply the publican a fair price for his property. He referred to old-age pensions, the importance of improving the'home life of the peeple. the influence of trade unions, and the agitation for the eight- hour day. With regard to industrial disputes, he pro. posed that there should he in every great centre an im- partial tribunal nf•arbitration, having the dignity of a. court of law. He was averse from a universal eight- hour day, but thought the expe,iment might be tried in respect of miners and shop assistants. Concluding, he spoke on alien immigration and emplryers' liability.

WOOLSTON SCHOOLS. totiSeptember 11th, the children attending the Watelston Schools were examined in scripture by the Diosesan Inspector, and the following is the text of his report:—" The children are in excellent order, and the high standard of religious knowledge prev- iously reached is well maintained. It is a matter of satisfaction to find that in so small a school, where the standards have to be so much grouped, it has been founa practicable to teach an important part of the Prayer Book, besides the usual catechism. All the work of the higher group is of the best, the younger children do well in New Testament and catechism, and more than well in Old Testament. Hymns are very well sung." SHEPHERDRY AT WARRINGTON. On Saturday afternoon, the quarterly meeting o, the Loyal Order of Ancient Shepherds' Warrington district, was held in the Friend in Need Lodge, Crow Inn, Bridge-street. The chair was occupied by Bro. W. ()leave. and he was supported by his deputy, Bro. T. C. Duckworth, Bro. James Parr, Bro. Eckersley, Bro. Bennion, and district secretary,Bro. P. Edgar, and other delegates and lodge representa- tives for the Warrington district. After the read- ing and passing of the quarter's accounts, and other routine business had been disposed of,P.P.C.S. James Lowe presented Bro. James Gresty with a handsome marble timepiece in the name of the district, upon his retiring from the office of Chiet Shepherd which he had held during the year 1893-4 Bro. Lowe in a few well-chosen words made the presentation, and words of encomium were also given by Bros. Box, Eckersley, Weir, Parr, Graham, and Ditcbtield. Bro. Gresty in rising to reply was warmly 'received. He thanked them for the kindly manner in which they had spoken of his services. The time piece with which he had been presented would be treasured by himself and his family very highly. THE " PELICAN " LICENSE TRANSFER. r. Hatton (Messrs. R. Davies and Co.) applied at the Warrington Borough Court, on Monday for the transfer of the license of the " Pelican " -)m John Allen to Fairfield Turner, to whom tern- rary authority was given on the 6th October nn- the present transfer sessions. He said that the magistrates were aware that at the adjourned licensing sessions the renewal of the license was :refused, and an appeal was pending against this de- cision. The transfer of the license until the 10th October would not in any way prejudice the appeal. Provision was made to keep open the licensed pre- mises pending an appeal, and the Inland Revenue authorities would make an order after the 10th inst to carry on the business until the appeal was determined. Mr. Hewitt proved the serving of the necessary notices. The transfer was granted. The license of the Ropemakers' Arms, CrossleY-at., was transferred from Joseph Buckhardt to James Row- linson ; and Joseph Buckhardt was granted the transfer of the beerhouse license lately held by Thomas Broadhurst in Orford-lane. The off-licensed house, 1, Allen-street, was transferred from Mary Grounds to William Allcock. • WORKINGMEN'S GOSPEL TEMPERANCE MISSION. The first concert of the season in connection with the above-named institution was given in the Mis- sion Ball on Saturday evening, the proceeds of .which were in aid of the funds of the mission. Councillor Monks presided. He expressed his hearty approval of temperance, a cause which he believed to be one of the greatest questions and perplexing problems of the present age. It commended itself to the best thought and the efforts of all men who had the welfare of the nation at heart. The mission by those entertainments was doing a good work. Miss Dutton (president.) delivered an address in her able manner, and gave a sketch of the World's Women's Christian Temperance Association. The concert was an excellent one, Mr. J. W. Jowett acting as accompanist. A vote of thanks waspro- posed by Miss Dutton to Councillor Monks and to the artistes for their services. Mr. R. Storey seconded, and the motion was heartily agreed to. The singing of "God save the Queen" ended a suc- cessful concert. On Sunday evening the usual gospel temperance meeting was held, and waa Con- ducted by the Vice-President, Mr. John Drury. Thoughtful and earnest addresses were delivered by the Chairman, and Messrs. J. W. Tanner, J. Rigby, A. Horrocks, and W. Hampaon. GIRLS FRIENDLY SOCIETY.

A successful flower, fruit and vegetable show was held in connection with the bowling club at the Horse Shoe Inn, on Saturday. The show was open to all, and the entries numbered about 200. The exhibits were nicely staged in Ithe large dining and tea room, and some fine specimens were shown of window plants, dahlias, apples, pears, cabbages. cauliflowers, onions, leeks, potatoes. The judges were Messrs. H. Jackson,of Newton, and J.Norman, of Golhorne, and their decisions gave general satis- faction. The show was visited by a good number of people on Saturday evening and Sunday, and the following officers and committee are to be con- gratulated on their success with their first annual show in aid of the funds of the club, viz.: Mr. G. Brimelow (president), Mr. T.Oakes (vice-president), Mr. I. Maines (treasurer), Mr. W. F. Pennington (secretary), and Messrs. H. Shaw, C. Allen, J. Maines, sen., T. Houghton and J.Taylor (committee), by whom the arrangements were excellently carried out, assisted by Mr. H. Hill. MEETING OF THE WARRINGTON AMATEUR PHOTOGRAPHIC SOCIETY. The monthly meeting of the above society was held in the Museum and Library Committee-room on Tuesday night. nere was a fair attendance of members, Mr. H. N. Houghton presiding. On the motion of Mr. H. Pritchard, seconded by Mr. P. Dalton, Mr.D.Harrison was elected a member of the society. The rest of the evening was taken up by Mr. Browne, of the Zenotype Company, who gave a, practical demonstration of the new toning devel- oper now in the market. The Zenotype is a process for reproducting pictures in colours most suitable to their rendering. Mr. Browne has the reputation, according to the professional magazines, of having experimented with chemicals never before dreamt of. His demonstration on Tuesday night was a success in every way. The process is simplicity it- self, so simple, in fact, that, given the required pre- paration, a child might use it. With all its simplicity, however, the effects to be obtained by its use are marvellous, and the rapidity of its action, together with the beautiful results obtained, callea forth the approval and admiration of all present.— At the conclusion of the proceedings Mr. Houghton on behalf of the members, thanked Mr. Browne for his entertaining exhibition. BELLRINGING AT ST. PAUL'S CHURCH. The Liverpool Diocesan Branch of the Lancashire Association of Change Ringers held their monthly meeting at St. Paul's Church, Warrinoton,on Satur- day. The towers represented were Farnworth and Gaston, also the local company, whilst members of the Chester Diocesan Guild from Grappenhall and Daresbury were also present. Ringing commenced shortly after three o'clock with touches of Plain Bob College Single,Kent Treble Bob,and a 720 of Canter- bury Pleasure Minor. At five o'clock the members and friends sat down to an excellent tea, provided by the Rev. 11. V. Pigot,vicar of St. Paul's. Atnongst the friends present were the Rev. R. Greenall isGrappenhall), Rev. A. H. Townsend, Mrs. Pigot, Messrs. Smethurst and Chantry (churchwardens), and Mr. J. J. M. Moore (hon secretary). The busi- ness meeting was afterwards held, the Rev. H. V. Pigot presiding. The Chairman in his remarks,said the first words:be should say were words of welcome to the members of the association and his friends from Grappenhall and Daresbury. Ile was sure that learning change ringing was the work of a lifetime, and as a rule the outside public were not aware of the merit due to ringers in ringing a 'peal. The Rev. R. Greenall said change ringing might be con- sidered first, a scientific amusement; secondly, a means of bringing men together and influencing each other for their mutual benefit: thirdly, as one of the manifold branches of Churdh work, and, in his estimation, worth of respect. Councillor Smet- hurst and Mr. Chantry also addressed the meeting, after which a hearty vote of thanks was passed to the Vicar and churchwardens. WARRINGTON SAVINGS BANK STATISTIC. In the ennual return of Trustee Savings Banks for 1803, full details are given of the affairs of the Warrington Savings Bank, from which it appears that this bank was established in 1818, and that it at the present time is open for the business 17 hours per week on a portion of each week day. The bank has six officers, one unpaid, namely,Joseph Charlton I Parr (treasurer),and five paid officers,whose salaries' and allowances amount to £7OO 4s. per annum, the names of the officers and their salaries or allow- ances being as follows :—J. E. Davies (actuary), £400; T. Tunstall (cashier), £l5O and house rates, gas, coals and water; W. A. Proud (clerk), £80; D. Davies (clerk), £2O; Arthur Bennett (auditor),X4o ; and guarantee premiums, £lO 4s. Inclusive of the before-mentioned salaries the expense of manage- ment during the year was £929 6s. 10d., which amounts to 6s. 3d. per cent. on the capital of the Bank. The rate of interest paid to depositors is .E 2 10s. per cent, the number of accounts open 10,739. and the total amount standing to the credit of depositors was .£293,262, the whole of which is invested with the National Debt Commissioners with the exception of £B6l Is. lid., which was in the hands of the treasurer. The amount of invested separate surplus fund was £2,100, other assets £1.300. making the total assets of the bank £297.709 Bs. Bd. It may be added that there are 267 similar Savings banks in the United Kingdom, of which there are 199 in England and Wales, 50 in Scotland, 16 in Ireland, and two in the Channel Islands. At the end of the last year there were 1,471,146 aecounts open, and the amount standing to the credit of depositors was £42,225,800. SILVER WEDDING FESTIVITIES AT WALTON. THE IRON TRADE IN THE NORTH.

STOPPAGE OF THE DARLINGTON STEEL AND IRON COMPANY'S WORKS. After an existence of 35 years the Darlington Steel and Iron Company's works were on Saturday closed owing to bad trade. At the annual meeting of the Company on July 30, the Chairman (Mr. T. Hugh Bel', of Middles- borough) stated that in consequeaoe of the general depression in trade the directors had come to the emu- elusion to close the works, but in spite of this declara- tion hope was not altogether abandoned that some ar- rangement might be naade for continuing the operations ,of the company. For the last few weeks, however, all ' that has been done has been to complete the orders t len running, and use up the materials in stock, and on Saturday an absolute cessation of work took place, w, t no prospect of a resumption. The career of the company is in many respects remarkable. The works at Alber: Hill, Darlington were founded by the late Wm. Barn- ingham, originally a blacksmith at Shildon, afterwards an ironmaster near Manchester, who bought land at Darlington and conveyed there part of the roofing of the Manchester Exhibition, utilising it for the roof of the great rolling mills which he opened at Darlington in 1859. For a dozen years or so afterwards there was great activity at the works,7o,ooo tons of iron rails being turned out yearly—probably more than are now made in the whole count' y. Twenty years ago the works were sold to the Da..lington Iron Company, and were then believed to make a greater quantity of rails than any other English concern. The course of the iron trade of the North of England at that time was largely shaped by the iron rail manufacture. In 1873 the associated makers of the North sold over 324,000 tons of iron rails, whereas now scarcely 1,000 tons are sold by them. This is due partly to the fact that steel rails are made instead of iron, and partly to the falling off in shipments to other countries. In 1872 the United States alone bought 970,000 tons of iron rails, but the quantity shrank yearly until in 1878 only 157,000 tons were shipped, and now the rail trade with America is tardy existent. The collapse of the iron rail trade and the decay of the rail trade with America and Russia were severely felt by the Darlington Iron Company. and it began to produce steel rails and other forms of iron and steel. In the attempt to keep pace with the times the old Company sank all its capital, and in 1882 a new Company, under the style of the Darlington Iron and Steel Company, was formed, after the works had been closed for some time pending reconstruction. The story of the works may be briefly told. Up to 1874 or thereabouts it was prosperous, and iron rails realized £ll 4s. 4d. a ton, but in 1879 they had fallen to £4 18s. 3d. a ton ; and steel rails which in 1879 fetched £lO a ton, are now only about £3. In the last dozen years, the Chairman of the Com- pany states' £4,000,000 has been received for products of the works, and out of this £700,000 has been paid in wages to labour on the spot,whilst altogether £2,800,000 has been paid in wages to coalminers, ironminers, and others (including the ironworkers themselves), so that capital has had but a sparse return. STAVELEY COAL AND IRON COMPANY. REDUCTION OF WAGES. On Saturday notices were posted at the ironworks belonging to the Staveley Coal and Iron Company that the men employed at the various departments would receive 10 per cent. reduction from the 20th inst. The reduction will affect between 2,000 and 3,000 men. See- ing that the Staveley Company are amongst the largest iron producers in the country,it is expected that similar reductions will be made by other firms.

. - THE CONSTITUENTS OF A "SAUSAGE.'

--- In his annual report to the Vestry of Hampstead. Pro- fessor Alfred W. Stokes, the public analvist of that parish, says, under the head" Sausages," :—" A sample bought from a street stall was found to consist of—bread seven-tenths, fat two-tenths, and one-tenth by way of fresh meat, with a little seasoning and a little vegetable colouring matter. This last was added to cover the absence of meat. Such sausages are being sold in other districts of London just now. Till we get a legal dgfini tion of a • sausage 'it is doubtful whether the Food and Drugs Act will touch the vendors or such concoctions."

THE SCOTCH MINERS STRIKE.

The result of the Scotch miners' ballot was declared on Thursday. Only 6,296 votes were r2corded. Of these 5,000 were in favour of remaining on strike till the Federation terms were obtained. The "Scotsman" says the men in the wpstern mining district of Scotland have returned to work, and the strike is now confined to Fife, a portion of the Lothians, and Stirlingshire.

IVOTICE IS HEREBY GIVEN, that the next .1.11 GENERAL QUARTER SESSIONS of the Peace for the COUNTY OF CHESTER will be holden at the COURT HOUSE, NETHERKNUTSFORD, in and for the said County, on MON:DAY, the 15th day of October next, at Half-past Eleven o'clock in the Forenoon, when the Grand Jury will be empannelled. The hearing of all Appeals arising within the Hundreds of Bucklow. Macclesfield, and Northwich, forming the Knutsford District, and the trials of all prisoners com- mitted from such Hundreds, will take place at such Sessions. The Grand and Petty Jury for the Knutsford District must be in attendance at the Court House, Knutsford, at the opening of the Court on MONDAY, the 15th day of October, at Half-past Eleven o'clock in the Forenoon. THE Court will be Adjourned to the Castle of Chester, on THURSDAY, the l&h day of October next. at eleven o'clock in the Forenoon, when another Grand Jury will be empannelled. The hearing of all Appeals arising within the Hundreds of Broxton, Eddisbury, Nantwich, and Wirral, forming the Chester District, and the trials of all prisoners committed from the last-mentioned Hundreds will take place at such Adjourned Sessions. The Grand and Petty Jury for the Chester District must be in attendance at the Castle of Chester, at the opening of the Court on THURSDAY, the lath day of October next, at eleven o'clock in the Forenoon. REGINALD POTTS, Clerk of the Peace for the County of Chester. Chester, 24th September, 1894.

SALE OF WORK AT THE GYMNASIUM. __-- Mrs.Beamont opened a sale of work at the Gymn- nasium on Thursday afternoonw week the proceeds of which are to be devoted to- wards wiping out a debt of £250 at pres- ent owing on this most valuable institution. The sale was not of a very extensive character, the stalls all told numbering seven. These consisted of a refreshment stall, a flower and fruit stall, and five other stalls laden with a quantity of useful and ornamental articles. The stalls were draped with delicately tinted material and with festoons of arti- ficial roses connecting each stall, and crossing the entrance, a pretty and pleasing effect was produced The fruit and flower stall, which was in charge of Mr. and Mrs. Mottram, besides containing a miscel- laneous selection of the fruits of the earth,exhibited for sale a considerable number of birds, At three o'clock when the.s.ale of work was opened amongst those present were : Mrs. Beamont, the Mayor and Alayoress (Alderman and Mrs. Greenall) Mr. R. Pier point, M.P., Revs. E.C.E. Carleton, H. V. Pigot, A. H. Townsend, and B. Percival, Mrs. F. Monks, Mrs. Lyon, Mrs. Carleton, Mrs. Adams, Miss B. Dutton (Moore), Messrs. T. Morris, F.G.S., J. H. Fleming, Vere Davies, McGregor, D. Jones, Alottram, D. Tinnion, W. Dimmelow ( hon secretary), and Ferguson. Mr. THOMAS MORRIS after referring to the pleasure it gave him and the committee to have Mrs. Beamont to open the sale of work, stated that he should like to men- tion of a little error that appeared in their balance sheet which had been issued. It was there stated that as a result of the dramatic performances in aid of the Gym- nasium over £4O had been handed to the treasurer, but if they included property given to the institution by the Dramath.: Society the amount would be increased to about £75. Some of the members of the Dramatic Society were feeling a "wee bit" annoyed at the mistake but he could say on behalf of the committee that they were very thankful for the assistance the society rendered to the institution. He then asked Mrs. Beamont to open the sale of work, remarking that Mr. Pierpoint,who was an excellent ta lker' would speak for Mrs. Bearnont. Mrs. BFABIONT in declaring the sale of work open said she took great interest in the gymnasium, and she sin- cerely hoped it would be very prosperous in the future. She also expressed the wish that the sale would be a very great success. (Applause.) Mr. R. PlEap ow, M.P., said he had very great pleasure in saying a few words on that occasion,although he had no great pleasure in hearing himself spoken of as an admirable talker. A talker on those occasions was generally looked upon more as a bore than anything else. (Laughter.) If there was anything that had induced him to come that day, not that he wanted much induce- ment, it was the tact that the sale of work was being opened by his friend, and their friend, Mrs. Beamont. (Applause.) They were always glad to see her, not only on those occasions but on others. The object they had in view that day, he need hardly say was a matter of paramount interest to the people of Warrington. They all knew the value of gymnastics. We had not much to complain of in this respect, as they had abroad, because the youths in this country were happily always able to take bodily exercise in all sorts of ways, whether in gymnastics, football, cricket, row ing, and all sorts of in- door and outdoor exercises. Mr. Morris had just re- minded him that one of the uses of a gymnasium such as that was that it should be lent towards the physical education of the school children of the town. (Applause.) He did not think it had been made use of in that direc- tion previously, but this year under the new education act the training of ehildren in gymnastics by proper teachers and in a certified place approved of by the In- spector of the district, would count towards the time of school attendance, which time as they knew gained the Government grant. (Applause.) They were awarethat to confine children, boss and girls, exclusively to the study of reading, writing, and arithmetic or things of that sort, was not so good as when they gave them their reading, writing, and arithmetic and other studies coupled with a proper and judicious exercise of the body. When they arrived at healthy mind in the healthy bodies of the population they would have arrived at the great object they had in view. He need not say one word in praise of Mrs. Beamont, but he heartily pro- posed a vote of thanks to her for coming there that day. (Applause.) The Rev. E.., C. E. CARLETON seconded the vote of thanks. He thought the committee were to be congratu- lated in having Mrs. Beamont to open that sale of work. She gave her sympathy to every good movement in the town and also her purse—which was a very important matter. Ffer attendance at that sale of work was only one of the many kindnesses which she had put herself out of the way to perform. He asked them to acknow- ledge it by passing the vote of tbanks. (Applause.) The vote of thanks having been heartily accorded, the sale of work was declared open. The following assisted at the stalls :—lhe Mayoress (Mrs. E. Greenall),Mrs. T. Morris, Mrs. D. Jones, Mrs. Ferguson, Mrs McGregor (secretary of the Ladies Committee), Mrs. J. Hesketh, Mr. J. L. Whittle, Mrs. J. Mottram Mrs. James Fair- clough, Mrs. Parsonage, Mrs.E. Whittle, of Warrington, a member of the Liverpool Concert Company, gave two entertainments in an ante-room at the Gymnasium. " Dear Sir.—l am a poor hand at expressing my feelings, but I should like to thank you, Your lozenges has done wonders in relieving my terrible cough. Since I had the operation of" Tracheotomy " (the same as the late Emperor of Germany, and unlike him, thank God , I am still alive), performed at St. Bartholomew's Hospital, no one could pos- sibly have had a more violent cough ; it was so bad at times that it quite exhausted me. The mucus, which was very copious and hard, has been softened. and I have been able to get rid of it without difficulty.—l am. air. yours truly, J. HILL." 'MATING'S LOZENGES. The above speaks for itself. From strict inquiry it appears that the benefit from using Keating's Cough Lozenges is understated. Since the operation the only means of relief is the use of these Lozenges. WESTON. _ _.... .4. v ~ ~ TikitvEsT FESTiVAL---On 81ti1oS7, harvest thanksgiving Servites were toncineted at the Weston Wesleyan Chapel, serMobt beitt AeliVasrea by the Rev. W. George (in the ) morning) a'nd Mr. rt. Posnott (in the evening). The 1 interior of the building was decorated with fruit,flowers, vegetables, kc., in a most artistic manner, and much .1 credit is reflected upon the lady-members of the con- gregation, who were responsible for this work. The • ) services were of an exceptionally bright nature, the musical selections rendered by the choristers being much . enjoyed. On Monday evening another service was held, when the Rev. T. Brackenbury was the preacher.. " There were large congregations and the proceeds' which, reached a very satisfactory sum, were devoted to the trust fund. ,
